### **Chapter 7: The Smuggler's Road**

*A goods siding in the Fens, and the line north, Winter 1852*

**CHAPTER OVERVIEW**

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander, with interludes from Catherine and Percival. He wants to get north unseen and fast.

**Obstacle.** The Institute's watchers, the pace of freight, and his own conspicuousness.

**Turning point.** The watcher on the platform, and Gideon's reroute over the hills.

**Consequence.** The chapter ends at a warm inn fire the night before violence, with the reader knowing more than Alexander does (Catherine's state, and Percival's intent). That sets up Chapter 8\.

**Span and length.** Friday 3 December to Sunday 5 December 1852\. Target about 4,600 words.

#### **Scene 1\. Cattle and manifests**

**Scene summary.** They board at a goods siding in the gap between one shunting engine and the next, three men and a Highland courier among a dozen drovers doing the same, and nobody gives them a second glance. Gideon forges manifests on a crate lid, varying the slant of every letter so that no two entries match. A crate-marking boy called Davey Coop, twelve and chalk-fingered, runs errands for him, and Alexander watches Gideon slip coins into the boy's hand when he thinks no one is looking. He learns later that the boy's father is dead and his mother takes in washing, and that Gideon has been paying for him all winter. The scene establishes Gideon's hidden decency before his loyalty is asked for, along with the texture of how goods and people moved by rail.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants to board unnoticed.

**Obstacle.** The practical problem of passing as drovers.

**Turning point.** He sees Gideon pay for Davey.

**Consequence.** Alexander is reassessing Gideon.

**Location.** A goods siding in the Fens, with cattle trucks and a crate lid used as a desk.

**Characters.** Alexander, Niall, Ewan, Gideon, Davey Coop, and drovers.

**Weather and light.** A raw noon under flat grey cloud, with a wind off the fen.

**Mood.** Gritty and working-class.

**Date and time of day.** Friday 3 December 1852, about noon.

**Time since previous scene.** About seven hours, after a cart journey across Norfolk.

**Scene duration.** About thirty minutes.

**Carried forward.** They are dressed down from 6.6.

**Setups and plants.** Davey Coop (Chapter 25).

**Rules to honor.** Cattle travel south by rail in covered vans by the early 1850s, and drovers ride with their beasts; a forged manifest carries three extra hands easily.

**Target length.** About 600 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 7.1**

**Continuity in.** About seven hours after 6.6, after a cart journey across Norfolk. The party is dressed down from the armory: no signet, no coat of Edward's, plain drovers' wool.

**Continuity out.** Alexander is reassessing Gideon. Davey Coop is planted for 25.x.

**Gideon (returning).** Now working: quick, irreverent, forgettable. Forges the manifests on a crate lid with the slant of every letter varied so no two entries match. His good coat worn badly. He spins the stamped token when he thinks. He pays Davey in coins he thinks no one sees.

**First appearance: Davey Coop.**

* Twelve, undersized, chalk on his fingers from marking crates. Dockside quick.  
* Father dead, mother takes in washing. Gideon has quietly paid for him all winter. Alexander should learn this later in the scene or the chapter, not from Gideon.

**Alexander.** Watches, reads, says little. Sees the coins and files it. He is learning a class of person he has only met as tenants: working men and a man on the make.

**Niall.** Stands where he can see the yard. Never lets anyone stand at his back.

**Ewan.** The only real Highlander. His habit of calling Alexander "sir" would break cover. Have him drop it or catch it.

**Location.** A goods siding in the Fens. Cattle trucks between shunting engines. A crate lid as desk. Raw noon, flat grey cloud, wind off the fen.

**Rules in play.** Cattle go south by rail in covered vans by the early 1850s, and drovers ride with their beasts. A forged manifest carries three extra hands easily. About a dozen drovers board with them.

**Threads touched.** Gideon's hidden decency ahead of his loyalty (given freely in 25.x). Davey Coop (25.x).

#### **Scene 2\. The drovers' van**

**Scene summary.** A big, drink-loosened drover named Tam Rennick is harassing an old woman over a seat. Alexander does not fight him. He pitches his voice for the whole van, the way he has spoken at tenants' dinners since he was fourteen, and turns every face in the carriage against Rennick without once raising his volume. Rennick subsides. Niall watches with an expression Alexander cannot read, and later says only, "That's a thing I can't teach you." Ewan, settled against the planking, asks whether they'll see Rhoswen, and uses her name without noticing he has done it. The scene shows that the public skills of a duke's heir have field value, and it moves a small degree of respect between them.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants to keep a low profile and still protect the old woman.

**Obstacle.** Social. Rennick is big and drunk.

**Turning point.** Alexander's pitched voice turns the whole van against Rennick.

**Consequence.** Niall's respect ("a thing I can't teach you"). Ewan asks about Rhoswen.

**Location.** The drovers' van on a slow freight train heading north.

**Characters.** Alexander, Niall, Ewan, Gideon, Tam Rennick, an old woman, and drovers.

**Weather and light.** Night outside. Inside it's cold, with a stove, tobacco smoke, and the smell of cattle.

**Mood.** Close quarters, then tension, then release.

**Date and time of day.** Friday 3 December 1852, about seven in the evening.

**Time since previous scene.** About seven hours.

**Scene duration.** About twenty minutes.

**Carried forward.** Nothing.

**Setups and plants.** Rhoswen's name (Chapter 11). The public skill turned field skill (Chapters 19 and 26).

**Target length.** About 700 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 7.2**

**Continuity in.** Seven hours after 7.1. Night. The freight has been moving north all evening.

**Continuity out.** Niall's respect moves a degree ("a thing I can't teach you"). Ewan has said Rhoswen's name aloud again.

**First appearance: Tam Rennick.**

* A drover. Red-faced, big, drink-loosened. Loud Border Scots.  
* Harasses an old woman over a seat. He is a bully, not a villain, and he subsides when the room turns.

**The old woman.** Unnamed, cold, dignified. She does not thank Alexander.

**Alexander.** Does not fight. Uses the trained public voice he has used at tenants' dinners since he was fourteen: pitched for the whole van, never raised. Quiet baritone dropping lower and more exact as he presses. No half-smile here (that is for fear, and he is not afraid of Rennick).

**Niall.** Watches with an expression Alexander cannot read. His line: "That's a thing I can't teach you." Read it as respect, not correction.

**Ewan.** Settled against the planking. Asks whether they will see Rhoswen and uses her name without noticing. Alexander knows the name by now (4.4).

**Location.** The drovers' van on a slow freight train heading north. Cold, a stove, tobacco smoke, the smell of cattle. Night outside.

**Rules in play.** A duke's heir's public skills have field value (this is the first proof). Keep him in a drover's disguise.

**Threads touched.** Rhoswen's name (11.x). Public skill turned to field skill (19.x, 26.x).

#### **Scene 3\. The cup**

**Scene summary.** This is an interlude from Catherine's point of view, and it is where the reader learns what she has become. She wakes in the refuge room at dusk and notices, as she does every evening now, how long she has gone without remembering to breathe. The pulse of every living thing within the walls reaches her like rain on a roof, and she counts them without wanting to. Six weeks ago Hessian bit her, and she drank from his opened wrist. She thought she was saying yes to the Danube, to the passes, to every route in her atlas. It was the right yes to the wrong question, and she is beginning to be able to tell the difference. A cup waits on the tray. Calum has taught her to hold it with both hands and drink slowly, and never to ask where it came from, and she has not asked. She turns the looking-glass to the wall, because the grey face and the red-tinged eyes are not hers yet. Hessian comes in the evening with the atlas and calls her Elisabeta, and she lets the name land and does not correct him, because she has learned that correcting him costs her days. The scene gives the vampire rules a body, sets up the dramatic irony that runs to the Midpoint, and plants the cups.

**Perspective and goal.** Catherine (interlude). She wants to drink without thinking about it, and to stay herself.

**Obstacle.** The hunger, and the compulsion.

**Turning point.** She lets "Elisabeta" pass without correcting it, because correcting it costs her days.

**Consequence.** The reader knows she is a vampire. Alexander doesn't.

**Location.** Catherine's room in the refuge.

**Characters.** Catherine, Hessian, and Calum mentioned.

**Weather and light.** Underground, and dusk by her body clock. Lamplight and salt damp.

**Mood.** Eerie, intimate horror.

**Date and time of day.** Friday 3 December 1852, about half past three in the afternoon, dusk at Loch Maree (sunset about 3:20).

**Time since previous scene.** This runs parallel to 7.2.

**Scene duration.** About an hour, compressed.

**Carried forward.** The bite about six weeks earlier (mid-October 1852), off the page.

**Setups and plants.** The cups (Chapter 15). The mirror turned to the wall (Chapter 15). The distinction between the right yes and the wrong question (Chapter 23).

**Rules to honor.** Vampires breathe but can forget to. They hear pulses. Their skin is grey and their eyes turn red with hunger. She has no reflection problem, so the mirror is her own choice. A compelled yes still completes the Turning.

**Target length.** About 900 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 7.3**

**Continuity in.** Parallel to 7.2. Six weeks since the bite (about 19 October 1852). The last Catherine scene was 3.3, when she was still human.

**Continuity out.** The reader now knows she is a vampire. Alexander does not. This dramatic irony runs to the book's end.

**Catherine (POV).**

* She is now a full vampire. Grey pallor, cold body, sunken eyes, red tint rising with hunger, small fangs that lengthen with hunger. Auburn hair, green eyes, beauty mark near the left eye. She does not need cosmetics in the refuge, where no one is left to pass for.  
* Interior voice: associative and image-rich before the abduction, now sharper and cataloguing, with a split between the thought her face shows and the one it keeps. A new undertone intrudes: the counting of pulses, which she resents.  
* She touches the bite beneath her collarbone (cold, dark, where Hessian's pull presses).  
* Wants to drink without thinking and to stay herself. Her lightness is a tool now.

**Hessian.** Evening visits with her own atlas open on his knee. Statements, not threats. Speaks of what is "owed," "given," "yours." Low, formal, unhurried, possessive courtesy. He calls her Elisabeta.

**Calum (mentioned only).** He taught her to hold the cup with both hands, drink slowly, and never ask where it came from.

**Location.** Catherine's room in the refuge. Fine linen, a narrow bed, dark stone with salt damp, lamplight. The looking-glass is turned to the wall by her own hand. The atlas on the table.

**Rules in play.**

* She breathes but can forget to.  
* She hears every living pulse in the walls.  
* The cup must be fresh. Blood spoils within hours. The reader does not yet learn whose it is (the cages are in 15.x, bandaged arms). She has not asked. Do not answer it here.  
* A compelled yes still completes the Turning. It was a true yes to the wrong question (the Danube, the passes), and she is learning to tell the difference. Do not let her forgive herself yet.  
* She lets "Elisabeta" pass because correcting it costs her days (compulsion sessions that erase time).

**Threads touched.** The cups (15.x). The mirror turned to the wall (15.x). The right yes to the wrong question (23.x). Hessian's pull.

**Flags.** The outline says this runs parallel to 7.2 but stamps it 3:30 p.m. (dusk at Loch Maree, sunset about 3:20) against 7.2's seven in the evening. **DECIDED:** treat it as one shared evening. Catherine's dusk by body clock is earlier in the day than Alexander's night, and nobody notices.

#### **Scene 4\. The watcher on the platform**

**Scene summary.** At a junction on the northern line, where they must change trains, a man in a coat too good for a goods yard marks Niall across the platform and turns away a moment too late. Gideon sees him first. Within the hour he has changed their route. They will leave the line in Northumberland and cross the Cheviots by the old drove road, because a man who is watching railways will not think to watch sheep. The scene raises pressure and confirms that the Institute is observing the Sterlings, exactly as Silas planned in 1845\.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants to change trains unnoticed.

**Obstacle.** The Institute watcher.

**Turning point.** Gideon reroutes them over the Cheviots.

**Consequence.** The plan changes, and pressure rises.

**Location.** A junction platform on the northern line at York.

**Characters.** Alexander, Niall, Gideon, Ewan, and the watcher.

**Weather and light.** A grey morning with coal smoke.

**Mood.** Suspicion.

**Date and time of day.** Saturday 4 December 1852, about eight in the morning.

**Time since previous scene.** About thirteen hours.

**Scene duration.** About fifteen minutes.

**Carried forward.** None.

**Setups and plants.** The telegram to Percival (7.5). The drove road (Chapter 8, scene 6).

**Target length.** About 500 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 7.4**

**Continuity in.** About thirteen hours after 7.2. They are stiff, cold and travelling in daylight for the first time. The freight has brought them to York.

**Continuity out.** The plan changes and pressure rises. Gideon has rerouted them over the Cheviots. The watcher will send a telegram to Percival (7.5).

**The watcher.** Unnamed. A man in a coat too good for a goods yard. Marks Niall across the platform and turns away a moment too late. He knows Niall from the Institute's files ("the younger Fraser," the knife-thrower). He may know Alexander from the engraved Sterling portrait on Silas's desk.

**Gideon.** Sees the man first. Quick, decisive. He reroutes within the hour.

**Niall.** Goes still. His ginger hair is the tell. He does not let anyone see his back.

**Alexander.** Wants to change trains unnoticed. Takes Niall's direction (6.3). He does not yet ask what the watcher's interest in Niall means.

**Location.** A junction platform at York. Grey morning with coal smoke.

**Rules in play.** Silas's 1845 plan maturing: observe, learn which servants travel, who is watched. The watcher is looking at railways, not sheep.

**Threads touched.** The telegram (7.5). The drove road (8.6). The Institute at the railheads (Gideon, 6.4).

#### **Scene 5\. Percival**

**Scene summary.** This is an antagonist interlude from Percival Ashcroft's point of view. At a hotel near the Perth railhead, overdressed for the Highlands, Percival reads a telegram from the watcher, with his father's latest letter open beside it. The letter says to observe, record, and not engage. Percival touches the scar under his jaw with two fingers. He has a kelpie foal from Loch Maree in silver mesh, taken from the same water that humiliated him in 1845 and now on the road south toward him over Drumochter, and he means to deliver it to Manchester and let his father see what his own methods are worth. Now the Sterling heir is coming north with the knife-thrower from the cave. Percival decides that observation can be conducted at close range. The scene introduces Percival on the page, frightened of his father and certain his own plan is better, and it plants the convoy.

**Perspective and goal.** Percival (interlude). He wants to prove to his father that his own methods work.

**Obstacle.** His father's orders, and his own fear.

**Turning point.** He decides "observation can be conducted at close range."

**Consequence.** The reader knows Percival will close in, and that the convoy is on the Drumochter road.

**Location.** A hotel near the Perth railhead.

**Characters.** Percival.

**Weather and light.** A cold Perth night with rain on the window.

**Mood.** Nervous vanity.

**Date and time of day.** Saturday 4 December 1852, about nine at night.

**Time since previous scene.** About thirteen hours.

**Scene duration.** About twenty minutes.

**Carried forward.** The telegram from the platform in 7.4.

**Setups and plants.** The foal convoy (Chapter 9). His orders to take the heir and the knife-thrower (Chapter 8).

**Rules to honor.** Percival touches his scar under the jaw with two fingers. He has his father's letter: observe, record, don't engage.

**Target length.** About 700 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 7.5**

**Continuity in.** Thirteen hours after 7.4. Night in Perth. First time Percival is on the page.

**Continuity out.** The reader knows Percival will close in and that the convoy is on the Drumochter road heading for him.

**First appearance: Percival Ashcroft (POV).**

32. Athletic, handsome, deceptively friendly. Sandy blond hair arranged to look tousled. Blue eyes, charming at a distance and cold up close. A smile that never reaches them.  
* Absurdly overdressed for the field. High collars a fraction too high, to cover the scar under his jaw from Edward's blade at Loch Maree. Silver-headed cane. He checks his watch.  
* Theatrical ease that turns clinical. Inventory language: "specimens," "assets," "stock." Calls Alexander "Sterling" as calculated disrespect.  
* Tic: two fingers to the scar when composure slips. A heroic invented story ready for anyone who asks about it.  
* Interior voice: self-flattering, resentful, calculating, and underneath it frightened of his father's disappointment. The fear is real.

**Silas's letter (open beside the telegram).** Observe, record, do not engage. Silas is the ledger; Percival is the flourish.

**The convoy.** He holds a kelpie foal from Loch Maree (Rhoswen's grown young, Beag) in silver mesh on the road south toward him over Drumochter. He means to deliver her to Manchester and let his father see what his methods are worth. How and when the foal was taken is not in the outline. Leave it unstated.

**The decision.** "Observation can be conducted at close range." He will disobey the letter in spirit.

**Location.** A hotel near the Perth railhead. Cold Perth night, rain on the window. The Scottish line ends at Perth. From here it is coach and horse.

**Rules in play.**

* Percival's successes are always partial.  
* He refers to Alexander as "the Sterling heir" and Niall as "the knife-thrower from the cave" or "from Loch Maree."  
* Do not let him be a cartoon fop. He has real cunning and real fear.

**Threads touched.** The convoy (9.x). His overreach at Alwinton (8.1). The race for the seven sigils comes later, but he is the field arm.

**Flags.** The outline's plants say his orders to take the heir and the knife-thrower drive Chapter 8\. He is in Perth and the attackers are in Northumberland. He must have given the order by telegram. The outline does not say how the men at Alwinton found the party after the reroute. Simplest: a tail from York. Settle in 8.1.

#### **Scene 6\. Alwinton**

**Scene summary.** They leave the train at dusk and walk the last miles to a drovers' inn at Alwinton, at the foot of the Clennell Street road over the hills. The innkeeper, Josiah Webb, is broad and unhurried, and he knew Ewan's grandfather from the kelp trade. By the fire Niall gives Alexander the warmer side without comment and takes the draught from the door himself, a habit so old that neither of them notices it any longer. The reader does. The scene is a breath before violence, and it carries the romance through practical care.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants rest and warmth.

**Obstacle.** None visible yet. Danger is outside.

**Turning point.** None on the surface. The scene is a pause.

**Consequence.** A night of calm before Chapter 8's violence.

**Location.** Webb's inn at Alwinton, at the foot of Clennell Street.

**Characters.** Alexander, Niall, Ewan, Gideon, Josiah Webb.

**Weather and light.** Dusk with snow beginning, a hard cold, the Cheviots dark above.

**Mood.** Cozy, before the storm.

**Date and time of day.** Sunday 5 December 1852, about half past four in the afternoon.

**Time since previous scene.** About a day and a half. They left the train at Morpeth on Saturday afternoon, spent the night in a carrier's loft, and travelled Sunday by cart and on foot.

**Scene duration.** About an hour.

**Carried forward.** The reroute.

**Setups and plants.** Webb (the fire, and Gideon's purse in Chapter 8). The habit of the warmer side of the fire.

**Target length.** About 600 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 7.6**

**Continuity in.** About a day and a half after 7.4. Left the train at Morpeth on Saturday afternoon, slept in a carrier's loft, travelled Sunday by cart and on foot. Snow is beginning.

**Continuity out.** A warm fire and a night of calm before Chapter 8's violence.

**First appearance: Josiah Webb.**

* Innkeeper at Alwinton. Broad, unhurried, forearms like a blacksmith's.  
* Flat instruction, the settled patience of a man who has seen every kind of guest. Few words.  
* Knew Ewan's grandfather from the kelp trade. He recognizes Ewan and says so, plainly.

**Niall.** Gives Alexander the warmer side of the fire and takes the draught from the door without comment. The habit is so old that neither notices. The reader should.

**Alexander.** Rests. Notices nothing about the seating and would have thought it a coincidence. Lets his shoulders drop. The half-smile is possible here.

**Ewan.** At ease among his own people for the first time since the road began.

**Gideon.** Pays. His purse matters in 8.x.

**Location.** Webb's inn, Alwinton, at the foot of Clennell Street. Taproom and fire, stable, narrow stair. Dusk, snow beginning, hard cold, the Cheviots dark above. Sunset about 3:38, so half past four is full dusk.

**Rules in play.** The inn burns before dawn on Monday 6 December (8.x); Catherine's diary burns with it. Keep the room's layout clear: taproom, foot of the stair, narrow stair, stable, upper floor.

**Threads touched.** Webb. The warmer side of the fire (romance by habit). The carrier's-loft and Morpeth details are off the page.

### **Chapter 8: First Blood**

*Webb's inn, Alwinton, Northumberland, Winter 1852*

**CHAPTER OVERVIEW**

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants to survive the night and keep the others alive.

**Obstacle.** Five armed Institute men, the dark, and his own inexperience.

**Turning point.** He kills a man on the stair, and the fire takes Catherine's diary.

**Consequence.** He climbs over the Cheviots at dawn, marked by the kill and emptied by the loss. He's harder and quieter. That sets up the Scottish road in Chapter 9\.

**Span and length.** Sunday 5 December, evening, to Monday 6 December 1852, dawn. Target about 4,500 words.

#### **Scene 1\. Two sets of prints**

**Scene summary.** Niall comes back from the privy and says quietly that there are two sets of boot prints behind the stable that belong to no guest in the house, and that one man has stood in the same spot long enough for the snow to stop filling his tracks. Gideon counts exits. Ewan checks the horses. Alexander, who has never been in danger in his life, asks what they should do, and Niall tells him: stay close, and do exactly as you're told. The scene raises pressure and puts the terms from the yard into practice.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants to understand what the danger is.

**Obstacle.** Inexperience. He doesn't know what to do.

**Turning point.** Niall's instruction: stay close and do as you're told.

**Consequence.** The terms are applied, and pressure rises.

**Location.** The inn yard and the taproom.

**Characters.** Alexander, Niall, Gideon, Ewan.

**Weather and light.** Snow falling steadily. The night is dark, with the moon rising late.

**Mood.** Alert and taut.

**Date and time of day.** Sunday 5 December 1852, about nine in the evening.

**Time since previous scene.** About four and a half hours.

**Scene duration.** About fifteen minutes.

**Carried forward.** The calm of 7.6.

**Setups and plants.** The watch rota. The exits Gideon counted.

**Target length.** About 500 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 8.1**

**Continuity in.** About four and a half hours after 7.6. The calm has lasted through supper. The terms from 6.3 (obey Niall in the field) are about to be used for real.

**Continuity out.** Watch rota set and exits counted. Pressure rises, and Alexander knows only what he is told.

**Niall.** Comes in from the privy and says it low: two sets of boot prints behind the stable that belong to no guest, and one man has stood so long the snow has stopped filling his tracks. Reads ground and snow like a page. Positions himself between danger and Alexander. He gives the instruction plainly: stay close, do exactly as you are told.

**Alexander.** Has never been in danger in his life. He asks what to do and accepts the answer. The obedience is chosen, not forced, and it costs him something. No half-smile. Fingertips tap.

**Gideon.** Counts exits without comment.

**Ewan.** Checks the horses (four, bought or hired from Webb on arrival; the party came by cart and on foot).

**Location.** The inn yard and taproom. Snow falling steadily. Dark, with the moon rising late. About 9 p.m.

**Rules in play.**

* The terms: in the field, obey Niall without argument.  
* Who set the men on them: the York watcher's tail (7.4) after the reroute. Not to be explained by anyone tonight.

**Threads touched.** The watch rota. The counted exits. The horses (8.4, 8.6).

**Flags.** 8.3 says Niall put the knife in Alexander's hand "an hour before." This scene is at 9 p.m. and the stair fight is at 1:10 a.m. **DECIDED:** Niall hands over the knife at the midnight watch change, off the page or in the first lines of 8.2.

#### **Scene 2\. Men who want them alive**

**Scene summary.** They come after midnight, five of them, Institute field men with coshes and a pistol held in reserve. One is broad, with a scar through one eyebrow and a puckered mark beneath his collar. Higgins. He sees Niall on the stair and smiles. "The knife-thrower from the cave." They have orders to take the Sterling boy and the knife-thrower alive and in one piece, which is the only reason anyone lives through the next minute. The scene reveals that Percival wants the heir as leverage and Niall for his files, and it pays off the Higgins thread from the prequel.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants to understand who these men are.

**Obstacle.** Physical. They are outnumbered.

**Turning point.** Higgins recognizes Niall ("The knife-thrower from the cave"). Their orders are to take them alive.

**Consequence.** Percival's aims are clear: the heir as leverage, Niall for the files.

**Location.** The inn's ground floor and the foot of the stair.

**Characters.** Alexander, Niall, Gideon, Ewan, Higgins, and four Institute men.

**Weather and light.** Snow, and the dark after midnight.

**Mood.** Menace.

**Date and time of day.** Monday 6 December 1852, about one in the morning.

**Time since previous scene.** About four hours.

**Scene duration.** About five minutes.

**Carried forward.** The watch from 8.1.

**Setups and plants.** Higgins escapes (later books).

**Rules to honor.** Higgins is broad, with a scar through one eyebrow and a puckered mark under his collar from Niall's knife in 1845\.

**Target length.** About 700 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 8.2**

**Continuity in.** About four hours after 8.1. After midnight. Alexander is on watch or half-asleep near the foot of the stair with a knife Niall gave him at the watch change.

**Continuity out.** Percival's aims are clear: the heir as leverage, the knife-thrower for the files. Higgins now knows Niall is on the road.

**First appearance: Higgins.**

* Institute mercenary. Broad, thick through the neck, a scar through one eyebrow. A puckered mark beneath his collar where Niall's knife pinned his shoulder in 1845\.  
* Thick-voiced, tests words like a man testing a step. Calculating even in pain.  
* Knows Niall by the wound (he calls him "the knife-thrower from the cave"). Percival, who knows him from the files, says "the knife-thrower from Loch Maree." Keep the two phrases distinct.  
* In 1845 he prodded Rhoswen with a pikehead and laughed when she snapped. Alexander does not know this. Do not let anyone say it here.  
* He is the Institute source who can be turned in Book Two. Do not soften him. Let him leave alive.

**The men.** Four others. Coshes, a pistol held in reserve. Orders to take the Sterling boy and the knife-thrower alive and in one piece, which is the only reason anyone lives through the minute.

**Niall.** Goes still on the stair. His line of sight, not his voice, tells Alexander to stay behind him.

**Location.** The ground floor and the foot of the stair. Snow, dark after midnight, wet wool and lamp oil.

**Rules in play.**

* Alive: they are to be taken alive. Use that to shape their tactics (coshes, not blades).  
* Niall is known to the Institute as "the younger Fraser." That file does not mention Isla.

**Threads touched.** Higgins escapes and returns (Book Two). The Institute's file on Niall. Percival's orders.

#### **Scene 3\. The stair**

**Scene summary.** In the dark of the narrow stair a man gets past Niall and comes for Alexander with a cosh. Alexander has a knife in his hand because Niall put it there an hour before. He does not decide. His body remembers six years of lessons and ends a man's life faster than he can think, and then the man is lying still on the treads below him and Alexander is looking at his own hand. He lets the knife fall and leaves it where it lies. Neither he nor Niall will ever mention the knife. This is the first kill, and it must mark him. It is frightening, specific, and unheroic.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants to not be taken.

**Obstacle.** A man with a cosh on a dark, narrow stair.

**Turning point.** His body acts before he decides, and the man dies.

**Consequence.** The first kill. He is shaken and leaves the knife where it fell.

**Location.** The narrow inn stair.

**Characters.** Alexander, and an unnamed Institute man.

**Weather and light.** None here. Darkness, splintered banister, the smell of wet wool.

**Mood.** Terror and aftermath.

**Date and time of day.** Monday 6 December 1852, about ten past one in the morning.

**Time since previous scene.** About two minutes.

**Scene duration.** Seconds of action, then a minute of stillness.

**Carried forward.** Niall put the knife in his hand an hour earlier.

**Setups and plants.** The kill marks him (Chapters 14, 16, and 22, the decision not to kill Percival).

**Rules to honor.** The first kill must mark him. Violence has consequences.

**Target length.** About 700 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 8.3**

**Continuity in.** Two minutes after 8.2. The fight has broken up on the ground floor. One man has slipped past Niall and comes up the narrow stair after Alexander. Niall put the knife in his hand an hour ago.

**Continuity out.** First kill. He lets the knife fall and leaves it where it lies. Neither of them will mention the knife.

**The Institute man.** Unnamed. A cosh in the dark. He is not evil; he is a hired man who chose a bad night.

**Alexander.** Does not decide. Seven years of lessons (Oct 1845 to Dec 1852\) move his hand. Afterward he stands and looks at the hand. This must mark him: specific, frightening, unheroic. Nothing tidy, no speech, no immediate vomiting cliché. The half-smile does not appear (that is fear as camouflage, and he has no camouflage left).

**Niall.** Below, not here. He arrives after.

**Location.** The narrow inn stair. Darkness, a splintered banister, wet wool.

**Rules in play.**

* The first kill must mark him (14.x, 16.x, and above all 22.x, where he steps aside instead of killing Percival).  
* Violence has cost. The body remains on the stair. See 8.4.

**Threads touched.** The knife left behind (Edward's silver-edged case, 6.6). Niall's "the first one should cost" (8.5).

**Flags.** The outline says "six years of lessons." The lessons began autumn 1845 and it is now December 1852: seven years. **DECIDED:** use seven. Logged as an outline correction.

#### **Scene 4\. Fire**

**Scene summary.** A lamp goes over in the struggle below, and the old timber of the stable catches. Ewan and Gideon get the horses out through smoke. Higgins and the others fall back into the dark, one of them dragging a wounded man. Alexander runs back up the stair for his saddlebags and cannot reach them. The diary of Catherine's he took from the jewellery box, the one thing he carried that held her voice, burns with the upper floor. The scene makes loss irreversible and cuts the last physical tie to the sister he remembers, just before he learns that the sister he remembers is gone.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants to save the saddlebags and the diary.

**Obstacle.** The fire.

**Turning point.** He can't reach them.

**Consequence.** The last physical tie to his sister is gone.

**Location.** The stable and the upper floor of the inn.

**Characters.** Alexander, Ewan, Gideon, Higgins's men retreating.

**Weather and light.** The fire lights the falling snow orange. Smoke and heat on one side, cold on the other.

**Mood.** Chaos and loss.

**Date and time of day.** Monday 6 December 1852, about a quarter to two in the morning.

**Time since previous scene.** About fifteen minutes.

**Scene duration.** About twenty minutes.

**Carried forward.** The diary taken from the box in 3.2.

**Setups and plants.** The loss sharpens the Midpoint discovery (Chapter 13).

**Target length.** About 700 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 8.4**

**Continuity in.** About fifteen minutes after 8.3. The fight is still going below. A lamp goes over in the struggle.

**Continuity out.** The last physical tie to the sister he remembers is gone. Higgins's men are away into the dark.

**Alexander.** Runs back up the stair for his saddlebags. He passes the body twice. He cannot reach the upper floor. In the saddlebags is the diary he took from Catherine's jewellery box (false bottom, 3.2), the one thing he carried that held her voice. It contains the two uncrossed lines he read in October 1851: "Asked Alex tonight whether he ever wishes he could simply leave. He said duty, as I knew he would." and "I am so tired of waiting rooms." The fire is not his fault, and he will treat it as if it were.

**Ewan and Gideon.** Get the horses out through smoke. Practical.

**Higgins and the others.** Fall back, one dragging a wounded man. The dead man stays on the stair.

**Location.** The stable and upper floor of the inn. Old timber, quick fire. Snow lit orange. Smoke and heat on one side, cold on the other.

**Rules in play.**

* The inn burns before dawn on Monday 6 December.  
* What happens to the dead man is not in the outline. **DECIDED:** he burns with the inn. Alexander knows it. Do not dwell.  
* The Institute now has no reason to put out the fire.

**Threads touched.** The lost diary sharpens the Midpoint discovery (13.x). The Institute's pursuit. The horses (8.6). Catherine's voice, gone.

#### **Scene 5\. Snow**

**Scene summary.** The two of them kneel in the snow while the inn finishes burning. Niall asks nothing. After a long time he says the first one should cost him, and that if it ever stops costing, Alexander should tell him. Gideon, grimly, presses a purse on Webb that will rebuild more than the stable, and Alexander notices that the smuggler pays debts he could easily walk away from. The scene puts the romance at its most restrained, with care given as silence, and it deepens Gideon.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants nothing, and can't think.

**Obstacle.** Shock.

**Turning point.** Niall's line about the first kill costing, and telling him if it ever stops costing.

**Consequence.** He is steadied a little. Gideon pays Webb.

**Location.** The snowy yard beside the burned inn.

**Characters.** Alexander, Niall, Gideon, Webb, Ewan.

**Weather and light.** The snow is thinning, the embers glow, and the cold is deep.

**Mood.** Grief and quiet.

**Date and time of day.** Monday 6 December 1852, about three in the morning.

**Time since previous scene.** About an hour.

**Scene duration.** About thirty minutes.

**Carried forward.** The kill and the fire.

**Setups and plants.** Gideon's pattern of paying debts (Chapter 25).

**Target length.** About 600 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 8.5**

**Continuity in.** About an hour after 8.4. The inn is nearly finished burning. Snow is thinning.

**Continuity out.** A little steadied. Gideon has paid Webb. Alexander has been told what the first one should cost.

**Niall.** Kneels beside him and asks nothing. After a long silence: the first one should cost, and if it ever stops costing, tell me. He does not refer to his own history. Care given as silence is the register. No touch beyond a shoulder against a shoulder, if that.

**Alexander.** Wants nothing and cannot think. Calm on the surface. The stillness of anger turned inward.

**Gideon.** Presses a purse on Webb that will rebuild more than the stable. Grim, quick, unremarked. Alexander notices that the smuggler pays debts he could walk away from.

**Josiah Webb.** Flat, unhurried, few words. He has just lost the inn. He takes the money without ceremony. He may say something to Ewan about Ewan's grandfather.

**Ewan.** Sooty, quiet, shaken. Checks the horses again.

**Location.** The snowy yard beside the burned inn. Embers, deep cold, thinning snow. About 3 a.m.

**Rules in play.** Gideon's pattern of paying debts leads to 25.x, where he refuses payment.

**Threads touched.** The first kill's price. Niall's protectiveness. Gideon's debts.

#### **Scene 6\. Clennell Street**

**Scene summary.** Before dawn they take the drove road over the Cheviots on foot and horseback, the old track the cattle took for centuries, climbing out of England into snow. Behind them Higgins is already riding for the nearest telegraph. At the top of the pass Ewan points north, where the hills fold away toward Hawick and the Scottish line. Alexander looks back once at the smoke and does not look back again. The scene makes the border crossing a threshold of its own.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants to get out of England.

**Obstacle.** Terrain and snow.

**Turning point.** From the top of the pass Ewan points north toward Hawick.

**Consequence.** The border is crossed. He doesn't look back.

**Location.** Clennell Street drove road over the Cheviots, climbing to the border ridge.

**Characters.** Alexander, Niall, Gideon, Ewan, and four horses rescued from the stable.

**Weather and light.** Clearing at dawn, with deep snow on the high ground, sun on the ridge at about 8:20, and wind.

**Mood.** Stark resolve.

**Date and time of day.** Monday 6 December 1852, from about six in the morning to midday.

**Time since previous scene.** About three hours.

**Scene duration.** About six hours, compressed.

**Carried forward.** Higgins is riding for the telegraph.

**Setups and plants.** The Hawick railhead.

**Rules to honor.** The Edinburgh and Hawick line opened in 1849\.

**Target length.** About 500 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 8.6**

**Continuity in.** About three hours after 8.5. Before dawn, then the climb. Higgins is already riding for the nearest telegraph.

**Continuity out.** The border is crossed. Alexander does not look back again.

**Alexander.** Looks back once at the smoke and does not look back again. It echoes Niall's line in 6.6 ("it does not get easier with looking back"). Harder and quieter. He still has the blunt practice knife and the swallow handkerchief. He has lost the diary.

**Ewan.** Points north from the top of the pass, where the hills fold away toward Hawick and the Scottish line.

**Niall and Gideon.** Lead and watch the rear.

**Location.** Clennell Street drove road over the Cheviots, the old track the cattle took for centuries. Deep snow on the high ground, wind. Sun clears the ridge about 8:20.

**Rules in play.**

* The Edinburgh and Hawick line opened in 1849\.  
* Four horses rescued from the stable.  
* The party must cross into Scotland before Higgins's telegram reaches Percival's net.

**Threads touched.** The Hawick railhead. Higgins's telegram (9.1, Percival's convoy). The border as a threshold.

**Flags.** The outline sends them to Hawick and the Scottish line in this chapter. Chapter 9 opens at Drumochter. Confirm in 9.1 how they get from Hawick to the Drumochter road (rail to Edinburgh, then north). Keep Edinburgh a pass-through (see 9.1 flag).

### **Chapter 9: The Capture Convoy**

*The Drumochter road, Grampian Mountains, Winter 1852*

**CHAPTER OVERVIEW**

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants to reach Loch Maree fast.

**Obstacle.** Distance and snow, and then the convoy, which forces a moral choice.

**Turning point.** He chooses to free the foal, and Percival arrives.

**Consequence.** He ends the chapter with a free, injured foal, an injured Ewan, and lost days. He's proud and uncertain ("Would he?"). That sets up the slow march toward the frozen crossing.

**Span and length.** Tuesday 7 December to Thursday 9 December 1852\. Target about 4,600 words.

#### **Scene 1\. North of Perth**

**Scene summary.** The Scottish line carries them to Edinburgh and on to the Perth railhead, and there the railway ends. They hire horses and take the military road north over the Drumochter pass. Two days of hard riding in snow change something in Alexander. It is not quite certainty, but its nearer cousin: the knowledge that he can go on when going on is the only option. He reads the road the way Niall taught him and catches a thrown shoe before Ewan does. The scene shows competence growing in increments, never all at once.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants to keep up.

**Obstacle.** The snow, the cold, and his body.

**Turning point.** He catches the thrown shoe before Ewan does.

**Consequence.** His competence grows in increments.

**Location.** The railway from Hawick to Edinburgh to Perth, then the military road north over Drumochter.

**Characters.** Alexander, Niall, Gideon, Ewan.

**Weather and light.** A hard frost, and snow on the high passes. There are about seven hours of daylight (sunrise about 8:35, sunset about 3:35).

**Mood.** Endurance.

**Date and time of day.** Tuesday 7 December to Thursday 9 December 1852\.

**Time since previous scene.** About a day.

**Scene duration.** About two and a half days, compressed.

**Carried forward.** The fresh kill and the lost diary.

**Setups and plants.** Reading the road (Chapter 15).

**Rules to honor.** The Scottish Central Railway has reached Perth since 1848\. Nothing runs north of it until the Dunkeld line of 1856, so from Perth the road is by coach and horse.

**Target length.** About 600 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 9.1**

**Continuity in.** They crossed the Cheviot ridge on Monday morning (8.6). Hawick and the Scottish line, then rail through Edinburgh to Perth, and horses hired at Perth. The dead man on the stair, the burned diary and the lost days are all fresh.

**Continuity out.** Alexander has grown by increments: not certainty but its nearer cousin. Sets up the convoy.

**Alexander.** Reads the road the way Niall taught him. He catches a thrown shoe before Ewan does. He is harder and quieter than he was in Norfolk and sleeps badly. His body has toughened. Do not make him a sudden expert.

**Niall.** Leads. Says little, checks Alexander's hands and seat without comment.

**Ewan.** Has the local ear for weather and hooves and is briefly outdone by a duke's heir. His pride is intact.

**Gideon.** Keeps up and complains dryly about horses.

**Location.** The railway Hawick to Edinburgh to Perth, then the military road north over Drumochter. Hard frost, snow on the passes. About seven hours of daylight (sunrise about 8:35, sunset about 3:35).

**Rules in play.**

* The rail line stops at Perth. Nothing runs north of it until the Dunkeld line in 1856\.  
* The Scottish Central had reached Perth by 1848\. Do not commit to a route or ferry details unless you check them (the Edinburgh to Perth journey in 1852 went by the Fife line and the Forth ferry).  
* The Edinburgh and Hawick line opened in 1849\.

**Threads touched.** Reading the road (15.x, where he catches the second false turning). The fresh kill and the lost diary.

**Flags.** Edinburgh is Niall's city and the home of the MacDuffs (Isla is at Portobello). Book Two reveals the money Niall has sent since 1848\. **Keep Edinburgh a pass-through:** platform or carriage only, no named scene, no Isla, no MacDuffs. If Niall's manner changes, keep it to a look he does not let anyone see.

#### **Scene 2\. The convoy**

**Scene summary.** Ewan sees it first from a rise: three wagons, a covered cage, men in good coats and worse boots, and the hiss of galvanic cells in waxed-canvas housings. Niall goes very still. It is Loch Maree's machinery, improved. The mesh is finer and the winches are copper-lined, and the batteries are smaller and better sealed. The scene reveals the Institute's scale and its progress since 1845, and shows that every device has evolved from the prequel's.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants to know what the convoy is.

**Obstacle.** None yet.

**Turning point.** Niall recognizes the Loch Maree machinery, improved.

**Consequence.** The Institute's scale is revealed.

**Location.** A rise above the Drumochter road.

**Characters.** Alexander, Niall, Gideon, Ewan.

**Weather and light.** A bright, cold late morning with snow glare and the hiss of the batteries.

**Mood.** Dread.

**Date and time of day.** Thursday 9 December 1852, about eleven in the morning.

**Time since previous scene.** Continuous with 9.1.

**Scene duration.** About ten minutes.

**Carried forward.** The storeroom machinery from 5.1.

**Setups and plants.** The mesh, the copper winches, and the batteries (the device evolves through the series).

**Rules to honor.** Every device grows from the prequel's machinery.

**Target length.** About 500 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 9.2**

**Continuity in.** Continuous with 9.1. Thursday, about eleven in the morning, on the third day of riding.

**Continuity out.** The Institute's scale and progress since 1845 are revealed. Alexander knows what the convoy is before he knows what it holds.

**Ewan.** Sees it first from a rise, lying flat.

**Niall.** Goes very still, the whole body. It is Loch Maree's machinery, improved. He counts men, wagons and horses.

**Alexander.** Wants to know. Asks quietly. He has seen the dead machinery in the undercroft (5.1). Now it is alive.

**The convoy.** Three wagons and a covered cage. Men in good coats and worse boots. The hiss of galvanic cells in waxed-canvas housings. Finer mesh, copper-lined winches, smaller and better-sealed batteries. The open eye above a cage may be on the fittings.

**Location.** A rise above the Drumochter road. Bright, cold late morning with snow glare.

**Rules in play.**

* Every Institute device grows from the prequel's machinery (5.1 storeroom).  
* The convoy heads south toward Perth. The party heads north.

**Threads touched.** The evolving Institute machine (Manchester laboratory, Book Two). Percival's convoy (7.5).

#### **Scene 3\. The hitch in her stride**

**Scene summary.** Through a slit in the cage canvas Alexander sees a pale blue-grey shape, flickering between scale and skin with every shallow breath, and when it shifts its weight it favors one foreleg in a hitch it has carried since childhood. Ewan says her name under his breath: Beag, the injured foal from the cave, grown. Niall says what the mission requires. They cannot spare the time, and every hour spent here is an hour Catherine does not have. Alexander understands exactly what he is choosing. "Then we don't leave it as it is." He makes his first plan. Gideon's forged papers will stop the convoy at the Dalwhinnie ford as a customs matter, and in the delay Niall and Ewan will reach the cage. The scene tests Alexander's moral clarity and gives him the first command decision that is truly his own.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants both things: to save the foal and to reach Catherine.

**Obstacle.** Moral. Time.

**Turning point.** "Then we don't leave it as it is." His first plan.

**Consequence.** He commits, and the team follows his plan.

**Location.** The rise, and the approach to Dalwhinnie ford.

**Characters.** Alexander, Niall, Ewan, Gideon, Beag in the cage.

**Weather and light.** Bright and cold.

**Mood.** A decision point.

**Date and time of day.** Thursday 9 December 1852, about half past eleven.

**Time since previous scene.** About twenty minutes.

**Scene duration.** About twenty minutes.

**Carried forward.** Beag's hitch from the prequel.

**Setups and plants.** Alexander as planner (Chapters 12 and 21).

**Rules to honor.** Beag is the injured foal.

**Target length.** About 800 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 9.3**

**Continuity in.** About twenty minutes after 9.2. They are still on the rise, watching.

**Continuity out.** Alexander has made his first real plan. The team follows it.

**Beag.**

* Rhoswen's grown young, pale blue-grey scales, flickering between scale and skin with each shallow breath. She favors one foreleg in a hitch she has carried since 1845, when she was the smaller, injured foal. No speech.  
* Seen only through a slit in the cage canvas.

**Ewan.** Says her name under his breath. "Beag" is the name his people give her, and it echoes her mother's one line ("Not the little ones").

**Niall.** Says what the mission requires: they cannot spare the time. He is right, and he says it as Edward would.

**Alexander.** Understands exactly what he is choosing. "Then we don't leave it as it is." The synthesis instinct comes up: he asks what each of them can do. Gideon's forged papers will stop the convoy at the Dalwhinnie ford as a customs matter, and in the delay Niall and Ewan reach the cage.

**Location.** The rise, then the approach to Dalwhinnie ford. Bright and cold.

**Rules in play.** Silver burns a kelpie. She favors the foreleg the smaller foal favored (Supporting Bible).

**Threads touched.** Alexander as planner (12.x, 21.x, where he asks each person what they can do). Beag freed (ford, 9.4). Rhoswen's mercy debt (opens in Ch. 11).

**Flags.** The Supporting Bible says Alexander recognizes Beag by the hitch, but he never saw her. The outline has Ewan name her. **DECIDED:** Niall, who saw the injured foal in 1845 (prequel), knows the hitch. Ewan supplies the name. Alexander understands who she is from their faces. Logged as a small bible correction: not "Alexander recognizes her," but "Niall recognizes her by the hitch and Ewan names her."

#### **Scene 4\. The ford**

**Scene summary.** Gideon's manifests hold for eleven minutes, which is two minutes longer than Niall said they would need. Niall cuts the mesh. Alexander learns with his own hands how silver netting comes off a kelpie without costing her more flesh than it already has, and the foal screams in a register no horse could manage. A wagon slews on the ice, and Ewan's leg breaks under a wheel. Then a rider comes down the road at a gallop, absurdly overdressed for the mountains, with a silver-headed cane across his saddle. Percival Ashcroft looks at Niall and knows him at once. He looks at Alexander and says "Sterling" as though naming a minor inconvenience. The scene brings Percival onto the field and makes the rescue cost a body.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants to free the foal without killing her.

**Obstacle.** The silver mesh, the guards, and the clock.

**Turning point.** Ewan's leg breaks, and Percival arrives.

**Consequence.** The foal is free, Ewan is injured, and Percival is on the field.

**Location.** Dalwhinnie ford and toll point.

**Characters.** Alexander, Niall, Gideon, Ewan, Beag, Institute guards, Percival.

**Weather and light.** A low sun, ice on the ford, and steam from the batteries.

**Mood.** Frantic action.

**Date and time of day.** Thursday 9 December 1852, about one in the afternoon.

**Time since previous scene.** About ninety minutes.

**Scene duration.** About thirty minutes.

**Carried forward.** Gideon's forged manifests (from 7.1).

**Setups and plants.** Percival and Niall's mutual recognition (Chapter 12).

**Rules to honor.** Silver burns a kelpie. Beag shifts between scale and skin. Percival carries a silver-headed cane and is overdressed.

**Target length.** About 1,000 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 9.4**

**Continuity in.** About ninety minutes after 9.3. The party has taken position at the ford.

**Continuity out.** Beag is free. Ewan's leg is broken. Percival is on the field.

**Gideon.** His forged customs manifests hold eleven minutes (two more than Niall said they would need). Bureaucratic bluff. The slant of his hand varies letter by letter.

**Niall.** Cuts the silver mesh. Steady, exact.

**Alexander.** Learns with his own hands how netting comes off a kelpie without costing her more flesh. Gloved or not, he handles silver near burned skin. An optional echo: open hands before Beag (the 5.4 gesture, Rhoswen later). She screams in a register no horse could manage.

**Ewan.** A wagon slews on the ice and his leg breaks under a wheel. He is 24 and does not cry out until it is done.

**Percival.** Arrives at a gallop, absurdly overdressed for the mountains, a silver-headed cane across his saddle. Knows Niall at once (ginger hair, the empty sheath he noticed in 1845, the files). Looks at Alexander and says "Sterling" as though naming a minor inconvenience. Do not give him his defining line here.

**Location.** Dalwhinnie ford and toll point on the Drumochter road. Low sun, ice on the ford, steam from the batteries.

**Rules in play.** Silver burns a kelpie. Beag shifts between scale and skin. Percival's collar is a fraction too high, covering the scar.

**Threads touched.** Percival and Niall's mutual recognition (12.x). The costly rescue. Ewan's splint (9.6).

#### **Scene 5\. Let them go**

**Scene summary.** Percival's men outnumber them. He could take them all, and he does not. He watches them get the foal clear and lets them ride, and the reader, who has been in his head, knows why: a Sterling heir riding north with a kelpie in tow will lead him straight to whatever his father's files have been circling for seven years. He lights a flare to tell his outriders where to follow. The scene makes Percival's schemes succeed in part, as they always do, and sets up his arrival at the camp.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants to get away.

**Obstacle.** They're outnumbered.

**Turning point.** Percival lets them go and lights a flare.

**Consequence.** They escape, and are followed.

**Location.** The ford and the road beyond.

**Characters.** Alexander, Niall, Gideon, Ewan, Beag, Percival.

**Weather and light.** The light is failing (sunset about 3:35), and it's getting colder.

**Mood.** Relief and suspicion.

**Date and time of day.** Thursday 9 December 1852, about half past one to two in the afternoon.

**Time since previous scene.** About ten minutes.

**Scene duration.** About twenty minutes.

**Carried forward.** Percival's intent from 7.5.

**Setups and plants.** Percival at the camp (Chapter 12).

**Target length.** About 600 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 9.5**

**Continuity in.** Ten minutes after 9.4. Percival's men outnumber them.

**Continuity out.** They escape, and are followed. Percival will arrive at the camp (12.x).

**Percival.** Could take them all and does not. He watches them get the foal clear and lets them ride. Alexander's POV cannot see why. The reader can (7.5). He lights a flare to tell his outriders where to follow. He treats it like an entertainment.

**Alexander.** Wants to get away. He sees only that Percival could have taken them and did not, and reads it as a puzzle, not mercy. Suspicion, not relief.

**Niall.** Covers the rear, watching Percival.

**Location.** The ford and the road beyond. Light beginning to fail. Sunset about 3:35, so the light is still good at half past one. Keep the failing light for the last mile.

**Rules in play.**

* Percival's schemes succeed in part and never in full. Here he loses the foal and gains the trail.  
* Silas's letter (7.5): observe, record, do not engage.

**Threads touched.** Percival at the camp (12.x). The flare and outriders.

#### **Scene 6\. The cost of mercy**

**Scene summary.** Beag cannot swim home from here, because no water runs from these mountains to Loch Maree. She must walk it, weak and burned, at a kelpie's pace on land, and so must they. Ewan rides with his leg splinted between two fence rails and refuses to be left behind. That night Niall says Edward would have done the same thing. Alexander asks, "Would he?" and genuinely does not know. The scene makes the price of mercy visible in lost days, and it plants the question of Edward that the reunion will answer.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants to know whether he did right.

**Obstacle.** Doubt.

**Turning point.** "Your father would have done the same." "Would he?"

**Consequence.** The question of Edward is planted for the reunion.

**Location.** A roadside camp in a pine shelter.

**Characters.** Alexander, Niall, Gideon, Ewan, Beag.

**Weather and light.** A clear, freezing night with a crescent moon.

**Mood.** Weary questioning.

**Date and time of day.** Thursday 9 December 1852, about nine at night.

**Time since previous scene.** About seven hours.

**Scene duration.** About twenty minutes.

**Carried forward.** Ewan's splint.

**Setups and plants.** Edward's answer (Chapters 11 and 13).

**Target length.** About 600 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 9.6**

**Continuity in.** About seven hours after 9.5. Night, a roadside pine shelter. The party cannot push the wounded, burned foal or Ewan any faster.

**Continuity out.** The question of Edward is planted for the reunion (11.x, 13.x).

**Beag.** Cannot swim home: no water runs from these mountains to Loch Maree. She must walk it, weak and burned, at a kelpie's pace on land. She does not speak.

**Ewan.** Rides on with his leg splinted between two fence rails and refuses to be left behind. Sooty, pale, stubborn.

**Niall.** Says Edward would have done the same. Sincere.

**Alexander.** Asks, "Would he?" and genuinely does not know. Wants to know whether he did right. He is proud and uncertain. He asks a question instead of answering one (Edward's teaching by question, and a corner he cannot turn).

**Location.** A roadside camp in a pine shelter. Clear, freezing night.

**Rules in play.** Lost days are the price of mercy (each day is one less for Catherine).

**Threads touched.** Edward's answer (11.x, 13.x). The price of mercy. Beag's slow march to Rhoswen. The frozen crossing (Ch. 10).

**Flags.** The outline gives a crescent moon at 9 p.m. on 9 December. The moon is at a thin waning crescent (age about 27 days) and rises only before dawn. **DECIDED:** no moon at nine; stars and frost only. A sliver may rise in the small hours if someone wakes.

### **Chapter 10: The Frozen Crossing**

*The Black Water gorge, Ross-shire, Winter 1852*

**CHAPTER OVERVIEW**

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander, with an interlude from Catherine. He wants to reach Loch Maree.

**Obstacle.** Hessian's reach, the compelled Calum, and Niall's secret.

**Turning point.** "Kill the knife-thrower," and the revelation that Niall knew.

**Consequence.** He rides west angry but still caring for Niall, carrying the birch bark. That is Pinch Point One. It sets up Chapter 11's arrival.

**Span and length.** Tuesday 14 December to Wednesday 15 December 1852\. Target about 4,800 words.

#### **Scene 1\. The raptor**

**Scene summary.** West of Inverness the road climbs toward the Black Water, where a narrow timber bridge crosses a gorge half-sealed by ice. A bird is quartering the sky above them, too large and too deliberate for any eagle Ewan knows. Niall watches it and goes pale. He has seen a feather like that before, in a Norfolk kitchen garden. He says nothing. The scene pays off the Chapter 1 feather and raises dread.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants to cross before dark.

**Obstacle.** The weather and the raptor.

**Turning point.** Niall recognizes the feather-bird and goes pale.

**Consequence.** Dread rises.

**Location.** The road west of Inverness toward the Black Water.

**Characters.** Alexander, Niall, Gideon, Ewan, Beag, and the raptor (Calum).

**Weather and light.** Heavy overcast and snow-light, with flat grey afternoon light.

**Mood.** Foreboding.

**Date and time of day.** Tuesday 14 December 1852, about two in the afternoon.

**Time since previous scene.** About four and a half days of slow travel with the foal.

**Scene duration.** About twenty minutes.

**Carried forward.** The feather from 1.5.

**Setups and plants.** The attack.

**Rules to honor.** A vampire can be abroad under heavy overcast. The raptor form comes from werewolf nature.

**Target length.** About 500 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 10.1**

**Continuity in.** About four and a half days of slow travel after 9.6, walking at Beag's pace over the Drumochter road and west past Inverness. Ewan is riding with his leg splinted. Alexander still does not know Catherine has been Turned (13.1).

**Continuity out.** Dread rises. Alexander has seen the bird and Niall's face, and once again gets no answer.

**Niall.** Watches the bird and goes pale. He has seen a feather like that in a Norfolk kitchen garden (1.5). He says nothing. Thumb on the palm scar.

**Alexander.** Notices the pallor and files the question, as he filed the feather. He asks no question; the unasked question is now a debt (6.3, 10.4).

**Ewan.** Says the bird is too large and too deliberate for any eagle he knows. He does not know what it is.

**The raptor.** Calum, in raptor form, scouting under Hessian's command. Calum hates this shape because Hessian made him scout in it. Do not reveal that his forms come from his werewolf nature (Book Three).

**Location.** The road west of Inverness toward the Black Water. Heavy overcast, flat grey snow-light, about 2 p.m.

**Rules in play.**

* A vampire can be abroad under heavy overcast.  
* Shapeshifting is not a vampire power (Dossier). Nobody on the page can say what the bird is.  
* Sunset about 3:15.

**Threads touched.** The feather from 1.5. The attack (10.3). Ewan's leg, Beag's pace.

#### **Scene 2\. The bridge**

**Scene summary.** Beag will not set a hoof on the timber. The bridge gives beneath the first horse, cut through by someone who knew exactly where to cut, and Ewan goes into the black water with his splinted leg. Beag goes after him without being asked, under the ice and out again downstream, and comes up with Ewan's coat in her teeth. Nobody commanded it. The horses are whistled back along the bank. The scene is an action scene that costs something (the bridge, an hour, Ewan's strength), and the creature's help is chosen, never commanded.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants to cross.

**Obstacle.** The bridge was cut.

**Turning point.** Beag saves Ewan unasked.

**Consequence.** They lose the bridge and an hour, and gain the creature's goodwill.

**Location.** The timber bridge over the gorge, the icy river, and the banks.

**Characters.** Alexander, Niall, Gideon, Ewan, Beag.

**Weather and light.** Snow is beginning. The water is black, with ice on the edges.

**Mood.** Peril.

**Date and time of day.** Tuesday 14 December 1852, about quarter to three.

**Time since previous scene.** About forty-five minutes.

**Scene duration.** About twenty minutes.

**Carried forward.** Ewan's splinted leg.

**Setups and plants.** Rhoswen's reception (Chapter 11).

**Target length.** About 700 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 10.2**

**Continuity in.** About forty-five minutes after 10.1. The party has reached the gorge.

**Continuity out.** The bridge and an hour are lost. Beag's goodwill is gained.

**Beag.** Will not set a hoof on the timber. Goes under the ice after Ewan unasked and comes up downstream with his coat in her teeth. Scales and skin, favors the foreleg when tired. No speech. Nobody commands her. This is help chosen, never commanded (Rhoswen's line in the bible).

**Ewan.** Splinted leg. Goes into the black water. Pale, half-drowned, still trying to speak.

**Alexander.** Takes command of the practical fix: warm Ewan, whistle the horses, find another way over. He does not thank Beag as one would a servant. Optional echo: open hands.

**Niall and Gideon.** Whistle the horses back along the bank. Gideon is quick and dry about the cost.

**Location.** The timber bridge over the Black Water gorge, half-sealed by ice; the river, the banks. Snow beginning. Black water with ice at the edges.

**Rules in play.**

* The bridge was cut by someone who knew exactly where to cut.  
* Kelpies live in fresh water and can take the cold that would kill a man.

**Threads touched.** Rhoswen's reception (Ch. 11). Beag's debt. The hour lost.

**Flags.** The outline says "someone" cut the bridge. It also puts the birch wood, where Calum appears, on the far bank, and never says how they cross. **DECIDED:** Calum cut the bridge under Hessian's command (to hold them at the gorge, then kill Niall). They cross downstream where Beag brought Ewan out, at a shingle ford, leading the horses. The outline is silent on both.

#### **Scene 3\. "Kill the knife-thrower."**

**Scene summary.** A young man walks out of the birches, slight, pale, and dark-haired, moving with the flat, borrowed steadiness of a body obeying another will. His voice is not his own. "Kill the knife-thrower." He comes for Niall faster than anything human, and Niall, who has never once been slow, is slow now, because he knows him. "Colie." For a heartbeat something fights behind the young man's face. The blow meant for Niall's throat opens his forearm instead. Alexander gets between them with Edward's sword and has no idea what he is fighting. The young man wrenches himself backward with a sound like tearing and runs, and a strip of birch bark falls from his coat. The scene is Pinch Point One. It reveals Hessian's reach into the far north and his hold over his own progeny, and it shows the true scale of the opposing force.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants to stop the attack.

**Obstacle.** A vampire under compulsion.

**Turning point.** "Colie." Calum turns the blow aside and drops the birch bark.

**Consequence.** Niall's forearm is cut and Calum has fled.

**Location.** The birch wood by the gorge.

**Characters.** Alexander, Niall, Calum.

**Weather and light.** The failing light around sunset (about 3:15), with snow.

**Mood.** Horror and confusion.

**Date and time of day.** Tuesday 14 December 1852, about quarter past three.

**Time since previous scene.** About thirty minutes.

**Scene duration.** About five minutes.

**Carried forward.** The raptor from 10.1.

**Setups and plants.** "Kill the knife-thrower" (Chapters 16 and 23).

**Rules to honor.** The Blood-Mark and Calum's compulsion. Pinch Point One.

**Target length.** About 900 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 10.3**

**Continuity in.** About thirty minutes after 10.2. They are across and sheltering in the birch wood, tending Ewan.

**Continuity out.** Niall's forearm is cut. Calum has fled. A strip of birch bark with a blood swallow lies on the ground. Pinch Point One.

**Calum (first sight for Alexander; first appeared in 3.3).**

* Slight and wiry, a hair shorter than Alexander. Deep pallor, dark brown curls, freckles gone ghostly, grey eyes. Hands closing and opening. Round-shouldered.  
* Under compulsion: the flat, borrowed steadiness of a body obeying another will. His voice goes flat and not quite his own. Faster than anything human. Only "Kill the knife-thrower" is spoken.  
* Fights the Mark: "a sound like tearing." He wrenches himself backward and runs. He turns the blow so it opens Niall's forearm instead of his throat.  
* "Colie." Niall may say "Nillo" only if it comes out as a break. Calum's Old Town Scots returns only after the compulsion.

**Niall.** "Has never once been slow, and is slow now." He does not throw. He does not fight back. This is the compelled betrayer meeting the man he failed.

**Alexander.** Gets between them with Edward's silver-etched sword and has no idea what he is fighting. He does not land a blow. The sight of the bloody swallow on the bark means his sister's mark is on this man's coat.

**Location.** The birch wood by the gorge. Failing light near sunset (about 3:15), snow.

**Rules in play.**

* The Blood-Mark: Hessian seizes Calum's will during active command. Silver driven into the Mark can break it (not yet).  
* Silver wounds a vampire. If the sword touches Calum, it wounds him. **DECIDED:** the blade does not connect.  
* Hessian's reach into the far north.

**Threads touched.** "Kill the knife-thrower" (16.x, 23.x). The birch bark (10.4, 13.1). Niall's cut arm (10.6).

**Flags.** The outline does not say which forearm. Niall throws right-handed (prequel). **DECIDED:** left forearm.

#### **Scene 4\. Four years**

**Scene summary.** The birch bark carries a swallow drawn in blood and a single mark that Ewan reads as the head of Loch Maree. While Alexander binds Niall's arm, he asks the question he now knows to ask. "You knew him." Niall does not lie. Calum MacDuff was his brother in everything but blood, who once dragged him out of a collapsed stair in an Old Town close. He vanished a fortnight after Niall went south in 1845, and four years ago Niall learned what had been done to him. "You promised me," Alexander says, very quietly. "Nothing I wasn't fit to carry." The chapter 5 promise breaks on the page. Alexander's anger makes him quieter, never louder. Niall, pale, says he will not run from this conversation twice, and Alexander says he is not ready to have it. The scene damages trust at the worst possible moment and gives the book's central relationship its lowest point before the Midpoint.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants the truth.

**Obstacle.** A broken promise.

**Turning point.** "Four years."

**Consequence.** The trust rupture. He isn't ready to talk.

**Location.** The birch wood in the dusk.

**Characters.** Alexander, Niall, Ewan, Gideon.

**Weather and light.** Dark with snow.

**Mood.** Cold anger.

**Date and time of day.** Tuesday 14 December 1852, about four in the afternoon.

**Time since previous scene.** About forty minutes.

**Scene duration.** About twenty minutes.

**Carried forward.** The promise from 5.6.

**Setups and plants.** The repair (Chapter 13, scene 2).

**Rules to honor.** Calum pulled Niall from the collapsed close stair. Niall learned the truth in 1848\.

**Target length.** About 900 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 10.4**

**Continuity in.** About forty minutes after 10.3. Dusk, snow, a small fire. Alexander is binding Niall's arm.

**Continuity out.** The trust rupture. Alexander is not ready to talk. The repair waits for 13.2.

**Alexander.** Asks "You knew him." Cold anger, lowered voice, every word placed. He does not shout. He says: "You promised me. Nothing I wasn't fit to carry." (A paraphrase of the promise in 5.6: "I'll not decide for you again what you're fit to carry.") He says he is not ready to have this conversation. He keeps binding the arm. Care and anger coexist.

**Niall.** Does not lie. Calum MacDuff was his brother in everything but blood, who once dragged him out of a collapsed stair in an Old Town close. Vanished a fortnight after Niall went south in 1845\. Four years ago, in winter 1848 (the night of his mother's burial), Niall learned what had been done to him. Pale. Says he will not run from this conversation twice, then stops. The burr thickens.

**Niall still withholds (Book Two).** The money to the MacDuffs and to Portobello, Isla, the notebook cipher, and what he feels. The rib scar is a story for 18.2. He must not lie about any of it if asked, and Alexander must not ask.

**The birch bark.** A swallow drawn in blood and a single mark Ewan reads as the head of Loch Maree. Alexander keeps it.

**Ewan.** Reads the mark. Wet, shaking, quietly useful.

**Location.** The birch wood in the dusk. Dark with snow.

**Rules in play.**

* Niall learned the truth in 1848\. Calum was taken a fortnight after Niall left Edinburgh in 1845\.  
* Calum is a vampire, Turned against his will. Alexander does not yet know Catherine has been Turned (13.1). He may fear it.  
* "Twice" in "run from this conversation twice": read as 1848, when Niall obeyed Calum's warning and told no one.

**Threads touched.** The broken promise (5.6, 6.3). The repair (13.2). Birch bark (13.1).

#### **Scene 5\. Keep leaving marks**

**Scene summary.** This interlude is from Catherine's point of view. Calum comes back to the refuge shaking, with one hand pressed flat over his heart. The Mark is burning where Hessian's command ran through it, and she can hear his heartbeat stumble. She gave him the birch bark days ago, and he carried it south under a command to kill and let it fall on purpose. "Go carefully, lass," he says. "Whatever you send north, send it soon. I'll not always be standing near enough to carry it." She takes his shaking hands in her cold ones and does not name what she sees in his face. The scene shows Calum choosing resistance in small ways and advances the bond between Catherine and Calum without letting it tip into romance.

**Perspective and goal.** Catherine (interlude). She wants to know if her message got through.

**Obstacle.** The Mark's toll on Calum.

**Turning point.** Calum reveals he let the bark fall on purpose.

**Consequence.** Their bond deepens.

**Location.** Catherine's room in the refuge.

**Characters.** Catherine, Calum.

**Weather and light.** Underground and night.

**Mood.** Tender and conspiratorial.

**Date and time of day.** Tuesday 14 December 1852, about nine at night.

**Time since previous scene.** About five hours.

**Scene duration.** About fifteen minutes.

**Carried forward.** The birch bark.

**Setups and plants.** Calum's resistance (Chapters 16 and 23).

**Rules to honor.** Their bond must not tip into romance. "Go carefully, lass."

**Target length.** About 700 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 10.5**

**Continuity in.** About five hours after 10.4. Night in the refuge. Catherine gave Calum the birch bark days ago.

**Continuity out.** The bond deepens without tipping into romance. Calum has shown resistance and has promised nothing.

**Catherine (POV).**

* Full vampire. Grey pallor, red tint rising with hunger. She hears Calum's heartbeat stumble. She takes his shaking hands in her cold ones. Touches the bite beneath her collarbone. She has watched her brother watch Niall for years and knows what she sees in Calum's face. She does not name it.  
* Interior: sharp, cataloguing. She does not let herself think of romance.

**Calum.**

* Back shaking, one hand pressed flat over his heart where the Mark still burns. Hessian's command ran through it.  
* Reveals he let the birch bark fall on purpose. "Go carefully, lass." "Whatever you send north, send it soon. I'll not always be standing near enough to carry it."  
* Old Town Scots. Apologizes before anyone accuses him. Hands are a binder's: careful. Do not make him brooding.

**Location.** Catherine's room in the refuge. Underground, night, lamplight, salt damp.

**Rules in play.**

* The bond must not tip into romance ("lass," no touch beyond hands).  
* Calum is a vampire, so his hands are cold too. A stumbling heartbeat means the Mark's command cost him blood or strength.  
* The bark was inked in her blood after the bite (13.1: it carries cold and the memory of an opened wrist).

**Threads touched.** Calum's resistance (16.x, 23.x). The swallow marks.

#### **Scene 6\. The road to Loch Maree**

**Scene summary.** They ride west in a silence that has a shape now: a bridge lost, a warning received, and a friendship's carefully guarded quiet broken open. Alexander keeps the birch bark folded beside the swallow handkerchief. He does not speak to Niall for most of a day, and at nightfall, without a word, he checks the dressing on Niall's arm himself. Neither of them mentions that either. The scene carries the romance at its most restrained after the rupture, with care surviving anger, and points the reader at Loch Maree.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants to reach Loch Maree.

**Obstacle.** Anger at Niall.

**Turning point.** He checks Niall's dressing himself.

**Consequence.** Care survives the anger.

**Location.** The road west through Ross-shire.

**Characters.** Alexander, Niall, Gideon, Ewan, Beag.

**Weather and light.** A clear, freezing day, then a starry night.

**Mood.** Tense silence.

**Date and time of day.** Wednesday 15 December 1852, all day and into the night.

**Time since previous scene.** About a night.

**Scene duration.** About a day, compressed.

**Carried forward.** The birch bark.

**Setups and plants.** Loch Maree (Chapter 11).

**Target length.** About 500 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 10.6**

**Continuity in.** About a night after 10.5. Wednesday 15 December. Ewan is on his horse with a re-splinted leg. Beag walks beside.

**Continuity out.** Care survives the anger. The ride points at Loch Maree (Ch. 11).

**Alexander.** Does not speak to Niall for most of a day. Keeps the birch bark folded beside the swallow handkerchief. At nightfall, without a word, he checks the dressing on Niall's arm himself. The tapping stops. Do not have him say anything.

**Niall.** Lets him. Says nothing. He has not been forgiven and does not ask.

**Gideon and Ewan.** Keep clear of the silence. Gideon's dry remark could be tempting, and should be resisted.

**Location.** The road west through Ross-shire. Clear, freezing day, then a starry night. Sunset about 3:15.

**Rules in play.** The two men have not spoken since the birch wood. The romance is restraint after rupture: practical care outlasts anger.

**Threads touched.** The birch bark. The handkerchief (18.2). Loch Maree (Ch. 11).
