### **Chapter 24: Dawn on the Heather**

*The northern coast, Winter 1852*

**CHAPTER OVERVIEW**

**Perspective and goal.** Hessian (interlude), then Percival, then Alexander. Alexander wants the dawn to hold what the night won.

**Obstacle.** Exhaustion, the dead, and a sister who can't stand in the light.

**Turning point.** The cairn swallows Hessian, and Catherine's pull falls silent.

**Consequence.** Alexander ends the chapter on the shingle beside Niall, his hand at the breast pocket steadying rather than checking. He is at rest. That sets up the homecoming.

**Span and length.** Thursday 23 December 1852, about half past eight to half past nine in the morning. Target about 4,300 words.

#### **Scene 1\. Hessian**

**Scene summary.** This is an antagonist interlude from Hessian's point of view. He comes out onto the heather as the first true sunlight crosses it. His skin blackens where it touches, and he counts the minutes as he has counted everything, knowing that thirty will finish him. He is starved and silver-scored and burning. The cairn rises out of the heather without warning, older than anything he has encountered in this cold country, its stones stacked by hands not entirely human. Its mouth is dark, and something rises out of that dark to meet him. He knows it. The narration does not say what it is. He drags himself inside out of the light, and the dark closes over him, and he begins to measure time by cold. The scene gives Hessian his ending in this book, not dead but drying toward a husk under the watch of the creature bound to him.

**Perspective and goal.** Hessian. He wants to survive.

**Obstacle.** The sun.

**Turning point.** Something he knows rises to meet him.

**Consequence.** The cairn closes over him and he begins to mummify.

**Location.** The northern heather, and the ancient cairn.

**Characters.** Hessian, and Josef (unnamed).

**Weather and light.** A clear, hard dawn with true sunlight at about 8:43, frost on the heather, and a low sun in the southeast.

**Mood.** A terrible, slow burning.

**Date and time of day.** Thursday 23 December 1852, about quarter to nine to five to nine in the morning.

**Time since previous scene.** About two and a half hours. He was trapped in the upper refuge and then driven out by the fire.

**Scene duration.** About twelve minutes, well under thirty.

**Carried forward.** The silver wounds from 17.1.

**Setups and plants.** The long revival (series).

**Rules to honor.** The thirty-minute limit, blackening skin.

**Target length.** About 800 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 24.1**

**Continuity in.** About two and a half hours after 23.5 and 23.6. Thursday 23 December, about quarter to nine. Sunrise was 8:43. Hessian was driven up out of the covenant chamber at about six (23.5) into the manor's cellars and upper refuge, and has held out of the sun inside the burning house until the fire reached him.

**Continuity out.** Hessian is inside the cairn, drying toward a husk under Josef's watch. This is his ending in Book One: not dead. The reader should feel the dark close and the counting of cold begin.

**Hessian (POV, antagonist interlude).**

* Wants to survive. Starved, silver-scored, burning. The concealment is gone: skin drawn and grey as old vellum over the bones, cheeks hollowed, eyes wholly black, fangs long. No pearl powder, no coat's antique correctness. He is a body counting minutes.  
* Comes out onto the heather as the first true sunlight crosses it: a clear hard dawn, frost on the heather, a low sun in the southeast. His skin blackens where it touches. He counts as he has counted everything, knowing thirty minutes will finish him.  
* The cairn rises out of the heather without warning, older than anything he has met in this cold country, its stones stacked by hands not entirely human. Its mouth is dark. Something rises out of that dark to meet him. He knows it. The narration does not say what it is.  
* He drags himself inside, out of the light. The dark closes. He begins to measure time by cold.  
* Interior voice: formal, unhurried even now, aphoristic. No monologue. The old contempt shows in a single thought about paper and doors ("Paper opens what paper can open").

**Josef (unnamed).** Present, not named in the scene. The thing that rises to meet him is a thin, colorless young man whose eyes hold nothing of their own. He speaks only if instructed; here he says nothing, or the barest word. He is human, so daylight does not harm him.

**Location.** The northern heather and the ancient cairn.

**Rules in play.**

* Thirty-minute sunlight limit; skin blackens fast (Series Bible). The scene runs about twelve minutes.  
* Silver wounds do not heal (17.1). He has burned blood healing (22.1).  
* A mummified maker's bond goes quiet and returns as the maker is revived (Series Bible, Main Bible). Josef feeds him slowly on his own blood. The pull at Catherine's bite stirs late in Book Three and returns in full in Book Five. Do not say any of it.  
* Do not name Josef or the cairn's builders.

**Threads touched.** The cairn (Books Three to Five). The long revival. Josef (19.1). The silent pull (24.4).

**Flags.**

* The outline says Josef is "unnamed" and the cairn's occupant is "the creature bound to him". **DECIDED:** Josef, unnamed, human, keeper of the husk (Main Bible). Do not add anything nonhuman.  
* Josef's whereabouts since 21 Dec (delivered terms at noon at the bothy, 19.1) are not stated. **DECIDED:** Hessian sent him ahead to the cairn on the night of the 22nd as a precaution. Not on the page.  
* The cairn's location relative to the manor and the ridge (24.2) is not fixed. **DECIDED:** within a few minutes on foot from the manor's north side, on the heather. Do not give a distance.  
* The outline says the fire "drove him out." **DECIDED:** the house he holds is the manor's cellars and upper refuge; the fire reaches him at about 8:40.  
* Hessian last appears in 23.5 driven upward at about six; there is no on-page account of the wait. **DECIDED:** none needed.

#### **Scene 2\. The ridge**

**Scene summary.** On the ridge above the burning manor, Percival Ashcroft sits his horse with an oilskin of rubbings buttoned inside his coat: one line of seven linked sigils, and no idea where the other six stand. He watches the house burn. His father's letter, unanswered, is in his other pocket. He says the name aloud, "Sterling," and it is no longer an insult. The scene gives Percival partial sigil knowledge, and completes his turn from dismissing Alexander to respecting him.

**Perspective and goal.** Percival. He wants to go home with something.

**Obstacle.** None.

**Turning point.** He says "Sterling" with respect.

**Consequence.** One of seven sigils.

**Location.** The ridge above the burning manor.

**Characters.** Percival, his men.

**Weather and light.** Smoke rising into a clear dawn.

**Mood.** Grudging awe.

**Date and time of day.** Thursday 23 December 1852, about nine in the morning.

**Time since previous scene.** Runs in parallel.

**Scene duration.** About five minutes.

**Carried forward.** 22.4.

**Setups and plants.** Book Two.

**Rules to honor.** He has partial sigil knowledge.

**Target length.** About 500 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 24.2**

**Continuity in.** Runs in parallel with 24.1. About nine in the morning. Percival left the manor's stair at 22.4 (about 3:45) with his men and, one way or another, the engine and the rubbings. The manor has been burning for nearly three hours.

**Continuity out.** Percival has one line of seven sigils and no idea where the other six stand. His turn is complete: "Sterling" is no longer an insult. The Institute has partial knowledge of the seven, and the race begins in Book Two.

**Percival (POV, antagonist interlude).**

32. Athletic, handsome, sandy blond hair arranged to look tousled, blue eyes charming at a distance and cold up close. Impeccably, absurdly overdressed, collar a fraction too high over the scar, silver-headed cane. He sits his horse on the ridge with an oilskin of rubbings buttoned inside his coat. Two fingers to the scar when composure slips.  
* Wants to go home with something. His father's unanswered letter is in his other pocket. He is afraid of what he will say to Silas, and the fear is real.  
* Watches the house burn. Interior voice: self-flattering, resentful, calculating, but the calculating is off. He does not retell his legend. He counts what he lost.  
* He says "Sterling" aloud, and it is no longer an insult. Inventory language may slip in ("assets") and fail.  
* Checks his watch once. The habit is intact.

**His men.** Two men, hired (22.4), one perhaps the ink-stained surveyor. Unnamed. They wait for his order.

**Location.** The ridge above the burning manor. Smoke rising into a clear dawn.

**Rules in play.**

* His successes are always partial (Main Bible). He has one line of seven linked sigils and no idea where the other six stand.  
* He does not know what the sigils are for. He does not know what Alexander did in the chamber.  
* His father's letter is unanswered. Do not say what it says.

**Threads touched.** The seven (Books Three, Six, Seven). Percival's respect (22.4). Silas's letter (Book Two). The Institute's siege.

**Flags.**

* The engine: 22.4 had it in the manor. **DECIDED:** he did not save it. It burned. He does not mention it here, or dismisses it in a clause.  
* The outline says the men are "his men" but the surveyor (17.2) is unnamed. **DECIDED:** one of the two.  
* The ridge location is not fixed against the croft ridge (21.1). **DECIDED:** a different ridge on the far side of the manor from the croft.

#### **Scene 3\. The debt**

**Scene summary.** On the loch shore at sunrise, Rhoswen lays Calum in the shallows, alive, and Beag comes out of the water to stand beside her mother. Alexander kneels to them both. Rhoswen presses her clawed fingertips to his temple and his throat, as she did at their first meeting, and then sinks into the loch without a sound. Ewan, watching from the boats, says the debt is paid, and Niall says it was never a debt she owed, only one she chose to honor. The scene brings the mercy thread to a close and restates that her help is always chosen.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants to thank Rhoswen.

**Obstacle.** None.

**Turning point.** The touch at temple and throat, repeated.

**Consequence.** The debt is closed.

**Location.** The loch shore.

**Characters.** Alexander, Niall, Ewan, Calum, Rhoswen, Beag.

**Weather and light.** Low sun and mist lifting off the water.

**Mood.** Grace.

**Date and time of day.** Thursday 23 December 1852, about quarter past nine.

**Time since previous scene.** About three hours since 23.6.

**Scene duration.** About fifteen minutes.

**Carried forward.** 11.2.

**Setups and plants.** Rhoswen's return in later books.

**Target length.** About 600 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 24.3**

**Continuity in.** About fifteen minutes after 24.1 and 24.2 began, and about three hours after 23.6. About quarter past nine. Alexander, Niall and Seraphina climbed out through the burning house at about half past six and reached the loch shore, where Ewan waits with the boats. Rhoswen carried Calum and Catherine into the loch caves at about a quarter past six and did not surface again until now.

**Continuity out.** The mercy debt is closed. Calum is alive. Rhoswen's help is restated as chosen, never owed.

**Alexander (POV).**

* Wants to thank Rhoswen. Kneels on the stones with his hands open, as at 5.4, 9.4, 11.2 and 21.3. He does not speak first. Quiet baritone. The half-smile absent.  
* Rhoswen lays Calum in the shallows, alive. Beag comes out of the water to stand beside her mother. Alexander kneels to them both.  
* Rhoswen presses her clawed fingertips to his temple and his throat, as she did at their first meeting (the old scars at Edward's temple and the pulse in his throat, Supporting Bible), and then sinks into the loch without a sound.

**Rhoswen.** In water-horse form: a great scaled neck, a mane of weed and coarse black strands, silver eyes. Or in her human form: a woman neither young nor old, sharp strange features, silver eyes, fine scales at the temples and backs of the hands, silver burns across her palms from the Ashcroft chain. No speech. **DECIDED:** water-horse form at the shallows, and her human hand for the touch. The change is not narrated; she has a hand when she reaches.

**Beag.** Pale blue-grey scales, grown; a hitch in her stride when tired. She shifts between scale and skin. No speech.

**Ewan.** On the boats, cap turned once in his hands. Splinted leg. Says the debt is paid. Soft Highland burr.

**Niall.** Grey, ribs bound. Says it was never a debt she owed, only one she chose to honor.

**Calum.** In the shallows, alive, pale, silver scar forming where the Mark was. Eyes closed or barely open. Old Town Scots if he speaks: "Lass" (asking for Catherine).

**Location.** The loch shore. Low sun, mist lifting off the water.

**Rules in play.**

* Rhoswen's help is chosen and limited. She is not a mount or a pet (Supporting Bible).  
* Sunlight burns a vampire in thirty minutes. Calum is in the shallows at about 9:15, well after sunrise (8:43). **DECIDED:** the shore where she lays him lies in the cliff's shadow, and Ewan and Niall have a boat sail rigged over him within seconds of the landing. The scene is fifteen minutes, well under thirty.  
* The debt (11.2, 21.3) is a mercy debt. Its payment is the touch.  
* Beag comes out of the water to stand beside her mother (Supporting Bible).

**Threads touched.** Rhoswen's return in later books. The open-hands gesture (5.4, 9.4, 11.2, 21.3). Calum's recovery (25.6). The bodies in the flooded refuge (26.x).

**Flags.**

* The Supporting Bible says the touch is given "at their first meeting and again when the debt is honored." **DECIDED:** this is the "again." The first was at 11.2.  
* Calum's sun exposure: see rules. This is a deliberate fix, not in the outline.  
* Whether Rhoswen speaks: **DECIDED:** no. True speech is reserved for the greatest stakes; this one is grace, not stakes.  
* The loch and the manor's coast: **DECIDED:** do not give distances between them.  
* Alexander, Niall, Seraphina's route out of the burning house is not shown. **DECIDED:** they reach the shore by about half past seven and wait for two hours. Do not put the wait on the page.

#### **Scene 4\. Silence**

**Scene summary.** In the loch cave where Rhoswen left her, out of the sun, Catherine sits with her hand on the bite. For the first time in two months the pull is gone. The cold is still there, but nothing presses on it. She tells Alexander when he finds her, and he sits down beside her in the dark and they say nothing for a long time. The scene establishes the silence that falls at Hessian's defeat. That silence is the baseline later books will measure his return against.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants his sister.

**Obstacle.** None.

**Turning point.** "It's gone quiet."

**Consequence.** The baseline silence.

**Location.** The loch cave.

**Characters.** Alexander, Catherine.

**Weather and light.** Dim inside the cave, and sunlit water at its mouth.

**Mood.** Peace.

**Date and time of day.** Thursday 23 December 1852, about half past nine.

**Time since previous scene.** About fifteen minutes.

**Scene duration.** About twenty minutes.

**Carried forward.** 23.2.

**Setups and plants.** The pull returns across the series.

**Rules to honor.** The pull is silent after his defeat.

**Target length.** About 600 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 24.4**

**Continuity in.** About fifteen minutes after 24.3. About half past nine. Alexander goes down into the loch cave where Rhoswen left Catherine. Sunlit water is at the cave's mouth. Catherine sits deep in the dark.

**Continuity out.** The baseline silence is set. The pull is gone; the cold remains. Alexander sits beside her and they say nothing for a long time.

**Catherine.** Grey pallor, the beauty mark by her left eye, auburn hair loose. Sits with her hand on the bite through her collar. Her voice is quiet, light, present. For the first time in two months nothing presses on the cold. "It's gone quiet." (Outline's turning-point line.) She may call him "Alex."

* The bite stays cold and dark (Bible). Only the pull is gone.  
* Her hunger is not addressed here. She has counted every pulse for two months; now the counting is a small hum.

**Alexander (POV).** Wants his sister. Finds her, sits beside her in the dark. Says nothing that needs saying. Hand at the pocket or not at all. Fingertips still.

**Location.** The loch cave. Dim inside, sunlit water at the mouth. She sits well back from the light.

**Rules in play.**

* The pull is silent after Hessian's defeat: a mummified maker's bond goes quiet and returns as the maker is revived (Series Bible).  
* The bite stays faintly cold and dark. It is the seat of the pull, which is now silent. **DECIDED:** she says only that nothing presses on it.  
* This silence is the baseline later books measure his return against (Book Three onward).  
* Keep her out of the light. The cave mouth is sunlit at 9:30.

**Threads touched.** The pull's return (Books Three to Five). The safeguard (23.4). The romance of siblings restored (20.3). Catherine's counting (Main Bible).

**Flags.**

* How Alexander finds her: **DECIDED:** Rhoswen or Ewan tells him where; he goes in by the cave's landward mouth, out of the sun.  
* Catherine's hunger: **DECIDED:** not addressed. She does not ask for blood.  
* Calum and Catherine were carried together (23.6). **DECIDED:** Calum was laid in the shallows (24.3) and Catherine left in the cave; Rhoswen took them to different places.

#### **Scene 5\. You'll do**

**Scene summary.** Beatrice comes down from the field, counting her dead. Sebastian arrives from the croft later than he needed to. Beatrice looks at Alexander for a long moment. "You'll do," she says, and she touches the brooch, and that is all she will say. The scene takes Beatrice a visible step toward conditional trust, stopping short of softening.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants Beatrice's judgment.

**Obstacle.** Her reserve.

**Turning point.** "You'll do."

**Consequence.** Conditional trust.

**Location.** The shore path.

**Characters.** Alexander, Beatrice, Sebastian.

**Weather and light.** A bright, cold morning.

**Mood.** Understated.

**Date and time of day.** Thursday 23 December 1852, about ten in the morning.

**Time since previous scene.** About thirty minutes.

**Scene duration.** About ten minutes.

**Carried forward.** 20.4.

**Setups and plants.** Chapter 26\.

**Target length.** About 500 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 24.5**

**Continuity in.** About half an hour after 24.4. About ten in the morning. A bright cold morning on the shore path. Beatrice has come down from the field counting her dead. Sebastian has come from the croft.

**Continuity out.** Conditional trust, stopping short of softening. "You'll do" and the brooch. Sebastian arrived later than he needed to.

**Beatrice.** Practical wool, iron-grey coil, the ward-worked brooch. Comes down from the field, her party bloodied, counting her dead (she lost two on the 19th and more now; **DECIDED:** the count is not given on the page beyond one figure at most). Looks at Alexander for a long moment. "You'll do," she says. Touches the brooch. That is all she will say. Never raised, never softened. Still Acting Commander.

**Sebastian.** Fair-skinned, prematurely grey, groomed, controlled. Arrives from the croft later than he needed to. Courtesy with something harder beneath. He may offer a phrase. He does not repeat "Until it doesn't." The letter (21.5) is not mentioned. Nobody accuses him.

**Alexander (POV).** Wants Beatrice's judgment. Stands, does not ask. Hears "You'll do." Files Sebastian's lateness as he filed the feather.

**Ellery.** May appear behind Beatrice, clipped, silent. Says how many she lost in hours and yards if she speaks.

**Location.** The shore path. Bright cold morning, frost, the loch, wood smoke from the manor far behind.

**Rules in play.**

* Beatrice does not give command (20.4, 21.4). "You'll do" is trust, not authority.  
* Do not say who gave the brooch.  
* Sebastian is a leak (Book Two to Four). No accusation, no clue beyond his lateness.  
* Beatrice's people were the diversion (22.2). Her dead are counted, not named.

**Threads touched.** Beatrice's conditional trust (20.4, 21.4). Sebastian's arc (26.x). Chapter 26 (the council). The Crown's response.

**Flags.**

* Beatrice's casualties in the assault (22.2) are not given in the outline. **DECIDED:** a small number; a single figure at most on the page.  
* Sebastian's lateness: **DECIDED:** he stayed at the croft with the wounded (21.5) and came down when the fire was seen. He says only that.  
* "You'll do" must not be softened by any follow-up.

#### **Scene 6\. Side by side**

**Scene summary.** Alexander and Niall sit on the shingle as the sun comes up over the loch. Niall's ribs are bound again. Alexander's hand goes to his breast pocket, and this time it is not a nervous check but a steadying one, the turn the gesture has been making all book. Niall writes something in his father's notebook in Gaelic and does not offer to translate it. Neither of them says anything that needs saying. The scene gives the romance its Book One resting point, two chosen equals side by side, with no confession.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants nothing more than this.

**Obstacle.** None.

**Turning point.** The breast pocket, now a steadying gesture.

**Consequence.** The romance's Book One resting point.

**Location.** The shingle.

**Characters.** Alexander, Niall.

**Weather and light.** Winter sun on the loch.

**Mood.** Stillness.

**Date and time of day.** Thursday 23 December 1852, about half past ten.

**Time since previous scene.** About thirty minutes.

**Scene duration.** About twenty minutes.

**Carried forward.** 20.5.

**Setups and plants.** Chapter 26\.

**Rules to honor.** No confession.

**Target length.** About 500 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 24.6**

**Continuity in.** About half an hour after 24.5. About half past ten. Alexander and Niall sit on the shingle. Niall's ribs are bound again. The sun is up over the loch.

**Continuity out.** The romance's Book One resting point: two equals, side by side, no confession. The gesture has turned from a check to a steadying.

**Alexander (POV).** Wants nothing more than this. In his father's coat, the signet in the inside left breast pocket (20.5), the watch and flattened ball in the other pockets (18.4). His hand goes to the breast pocket, and this time it is a steadying, not a check. He does not say anything about it. Fingertips still. The half-smile may return.

**Niall.** Grey, ribs bound with the swallow handkerchief (18.2) or a fresh bandage. Sits with his father's field notebook (13.6). Writes something in it in Gaelic and does not offer to translate. The burr thickens if he speaks. He calls Alexander "Alex" only if it is honest. He says nothing that needs saying.

**Location.** The shingle. Winter sun on the loch. The sea sound.

**Rules in play.**

* No confession. The romance is behavior (18.2, 20.6).  
* The gesture: check (20.5, 22.3), then steadying (24.6, 26.x).  
* Niall's Gaelic in the notebook: do not translate. It is a private line.  
* Niall's father is Alasdair Fraser, who died in 1838 for Edward (18.4). The ball in Alexander's pocket is connected to that. Do not surface it here.

**Threads touched.** The gesture (26.x). The notebook (13.6, Book Two cipher). Chapter 26\. The romance's resting point.

**Flags.**

* What Niall writes: not stated. **DECIDED:** unstated. Do not invent a line.  
* The handkerchiefs: Alexander's bound Niall's ribs at 18.2. Niall's own (Catherine's festival gift, 1.2) is kept in the notebook and appears at 26.5. **DECIDED:** what becomes of Alexander's is not settled; it may stay on the bandage or be laundered. Do not have Niall use his own here.  
* Where Catherine and Calum are: **DECIDED:** Catherine in the cave, Calum being cared for by Seraphina and Ewan under sail canvas. Alexander does not go to them in this scene.

## **PART SIX: THE HIDDEN CROWN**

---

### **Chapter 25: The House Without Them**

*Sterling Castle, Norfolk, Winter 1852*

**CHAPTER OVERVIEW**

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander, with the household. He wants to bring the family home and hold the house.

**Obstacle.** Grief, public secrecy, and the Institute at the walls.

**Turning point.** Harcourt's finding, and Pike's choice.

**Consequence.** He ends the chapter with Catherine planning a route of her own and the house held. Grief, with a future. That sets up the Crown.

**Span and length.** Friday 31 December 1852 to Friday 7 January 1853\. Target about 4,800 words.

#### **Scene 1\. Crape**

**Scene summary.** Black crape hangs over the gate. The staff stand in a line in the courtyard, as they always have, and the village beyond is silent along its whole length. Catherine comes home separately, by night, in a closed carriage. Alexander walks across the threshold with the line of staff falling in behind him, and understands that from now on the line forms behind him. The scene brings the book home, and makes the inheritance of the title visible in a single ritual of the household.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants to cross the threshold.

**Obstacle.** Grief.

**Turning point.** The staff line forms behind him.

**Consequence.** The inheritance is made visible.

**Location.** Sterling Castle gate and courtyard.

**Characters.** Alexander, Niall, the staff, Thomas, O'Brien. Catherine arrives by night.

**Weather and light.** New Year's Eve dusk (sunset about 3:54), black crape stiff with frost.

**Mood.** Mourning.

**Date and time of day.** Friday 31 December 1852, about half past three in the afternoon.

**Time since previous scene.** About eight days of travel south. Catherine travelled only at night by closed carriage.

**Scene duration.** About twenty minutes.

**Carried forward.** There are no bodies. Edward and Amelia lie in the flooded refuge, and the public account says they were buried abroad.

**Setups and plants.** The memorial (offstage).

**Target length.** About 700 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 25.1**

**Continuity in.** About eight days of travel south after 24.6 (23 Dec). Friday 31 December 1852, about half past three in the afternoon, sunset about 3:54. Alexander travels in his father's coat with the signet in the breast pocket (20.5), the watch (stopped) and the flattened ball in the other pockets (18.4). Catherine and Calum travel only by night, in closed carriages, separately from the daylight party. Beatrice and the Vanguard's people went their own way. Ewan stays north.

**Continuity out.** The inheritance is visible in one household ritual: the line of staff forms behind him. Catherine has come home by night. The public account (buried abroad) is in place.

**Alexander (POV).**

* Wants to cross the threshold. Grief, in the body: tired, exact, quiet. Baritone low. Half-smile absent. He is Duke in law (19.2) and has been for twelve days; today he is one in the household's sight.  
* Turning point: he walks across the threshold and the staff line, which has always formed in front of Edward, falls in behind him. He understands from now on the line forms behind him.  
* His hand may go to the breast pocket. Keep it steady, not nervous.

**The staff.** In a line in the courtyard, as always. Mr. O'Brien (sixty, spare, silver-haired, immaculately pressed, gives away nothing). Thomas Barrow (forties, big-handed, fair and freckled, coat too formal for the work he does in it). Mrs. Thompson (broad, floury, red-forearmed, hands folded hard in her apron because she has decided not to cry; she may say "love" once at most). Mrs. Ashworth (keys, pencil in her hair). Others unnamed. The village beyond is silent along its whole length.

**Niall.** At Alexander's side, one pace beside, not behind. Ribs still bound. He came south with him. Gaunt, dry. He says nothing.

**Catherine.** Not present. She arrives later, at night, by closed carriage.

**Location.** Sterling Castle gate and courtyard. Black crape over the gate, stiff with frost. New Year's Eve dusk, cold, clear or grey (not stated; **DECIDED:** clear and hard, the light going).

**Rules in play.**

* There are no bodies. Edward and Amelia lie in the flooded refuge (26.x). The public account says they died of a fever abroad and were buried there (25.3, 26.1).  
* Catherine cannot travel in daylight. Do not have her at the gate.  
* The staff are not read in on the Vanguard except Thomas and the senior household (see Supporting Bible). **DECIDED:** they know only that the Duke and Duchess are dead and the young Duke is home.  
* The line forms behind him. Do not explain it. Let O'Brien's stillness and the crape do the work.

**Threads touched.** The inheritance (title and standing, 19.2). The memorial (offstage). Thomas as steward (25.4). The public account (25.3). The gesture (20.5).

**Flags.**

* The outline says "about eight days of travel south." The journey from Wester Ross to Norfolk in winter by land and rail would take about that. **DECIDED:** unremarked on the page.  
* Whether Catherine's return is explained to the staff or the village is not in the outline. **DECIDED:** the household inner circle (O'Brien, Thomas, Mrs. Ashworth) know she is alive and home; the rest are told nothing this evening. Surface the public account for her return with Fox (see 25.3).  
* Ewan is not at the castle. **DECIDED:** he stays at Loch Maree (warden, later, Supporting Bible). Not on the page.

#### **Scene 2\. What the blood holds**

**Scene summary.** Harcourt has been waiting. In the hidden infirmary, before Alexander has taken off his coat, she tells him and Niall what she has found. There is a property in Catherine's blood she cannot identify and cannot dismiss. It suggests the disease might one day be driven back and Catherine made human again. It will not work alone. It needs some further agent, a preparation or remedy that would help Catherine's blood fight the disease off, and she does not know what that agent is. Then Catherine comes in, and Harcourt asks her, not her brother, for leave to go on with the work. Catherine sets her terms. Harcourt calls her "Lady Catherine" and writes the terms down. The scene gives the cure search its engine, complete: the finding, the missing agent, and Harcourt's arc toward personhood.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants to hear it.

**Obstacle.** Harcourt's uncertainty.

**Turning point.** "Lady Catherine."

**Consequence.** The cure engine starts.

**Location.** The infirmary.

**Characters.** Alexander, Niall, Harcourt, Catherine.

**Weather and light.** Night, and gas light.

**Mood.** Hope, carefully.

**Date and time of day.** Friday 31 December 1852, about half past five in the evening.

**Time since previous scene.** About two hours.

**Scene duration.** About forty minutes.

**Carried forward.** 12.4.

**Setups and plants.** The cure search in later books.

**Rules to honor.** The finding exactly as Harcourt describes it: a property she cannot name, suggestive of reversal, useless without a further agent.

**Target length.** About 900 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 25.2**

**Continuity in.** About two hours after 25.1. Friday 31 December, about half past five in the evening, full dark. Harcourt has been waiting in the hidden infirmary. Catherine arrived by night and has been settled below.

**Continuity out.** The cure search has its engine: a property in Catherine's blood, a missing further agent, Catherine's consent. Harcourt has called her "Lady Catherine."

**Harcourt (returning).**

* Mid-forties, compact, spectacles, sleeves to the elbow, hair scraped back going grey at the parting, leather notebook. Sharp, unsentimental fingers; catalogues a person the way she would a specimen. Exact period vocabulary: "property," "principle," "corpuscles," "contagion." Delivers truths unsoftened.  
* Her finding, exactly (Outline): there is a property in Catherine's blood she cannot identify and cannot dismiss. It suggests the disease might one day be driven back and Catherine made human again. It will not work alone. It needs some further agent, a preparation or remedy that would help Catherine's blood fight the disease off, and she does not know what the agent is.  
* The disease inference now rests on a post-bite sample (12.4 flag). **DECIDED:** she has had one from Catherine's arrival by night (a lancet, consented) and compared it to the pre-bite blood on the painter's brush (6.1, 12.4).  
* Turning point: she asks Catherine, not Alexander, for leave to go on. Then calls her "Lady Catherine" and writes the terms down. Her arc is toward personhood and consent (Dossier). At 12.4 she questioned the boggart child as a specimen; now she asks.

**Catherine.** Grey, poised, upright, powder and gloves. Lightness kept as a choice. Her terms, spoken, not bargained: she may stop at any time. Nothing is done to her without her leave. She sees every finding. No one else consents for her, not Alexander, not Harcourt. **DECIDED:** those four. Bible candidate. Voice bright, quick, warm; laugh may arrive early. "Alex" once.

**Alexander (POV).** Wants to hear it. Still in the coat (**DECIDED:** he has not taken it off; it is his father's and he wears it). Listens twice over. Asks precise questions. Does not decide for her. His flaw corrected (20.3).

**Niall.** By the door or at the table, ribs bound. Says little. He listens for cadence, not content (6.2). May ask one dry practical question.

**Location.** The hidden infirmary: steel tables, gas lamps turned up too bright for comfort. Night, gas light. Snow at the windows only if the room has windows (it is underground; **DECIDED:** none).

**Rules in play.**

* The finding exactly as described: a property she cannot name, suggestive of reversal, useless without a further agent. Do not name the agent, the cure, or the line.  
* Alexander's blood carries the same property (Main Bible). Nobody knows. Do not hint at it, and do not have Harcourt ask for his.  
* The further agent (holy well water from Isle Maree, prepared with rowan by the kelpies' method, Dossier) is unknown to all. Rhoswen's mother's testimony in Alasdair Fraser's cipher holds it (Book Six). Do not surface any of it.  
* No one knows Catherine is Hessian's descendant (Book Six). Harcourt must not guess.

**Threads touched.** The cure search (series). The brush (6.1, 12.4). Harcourt's arc: personhood, consent. Catherine's agency (23.2). "Lady Catherine."

**Flags.**

* The Series Bible says Harcourt finds the disease "might be driven back" after a post-bite sample; 12.4 had her find only an unidentified property. **DECIDED:** the disease inference comes here.  
* Catherine's terms are not in the outline. **DECIDED:** the four above, proposed for the bible: she may stop; nothing without her leave; she sees every finding; no one else consents for her.  
* Whether Catherine feeds before or after: **DECIDED:** not shown. She has fed by cut and cup (18.3); her supply is handled offstage (Book Two).

#### **Scene 3\. Pike**

**Scene summary.** Inspector Pike calls in his brown coat. He is given the public account of the deaths: a fever contracted abroad, in a country left unnamed. He listens. He looks at the young Duke, at the black crape, at the carefully tidied wall of a story, and decides he would rather not officially know where this thread leads. "I'll not close the file," he says. "I'll simply stop reading it." The scene completes Pike's chosen incuriosity.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants Pike to let it go.

**Obstacle.** Pike's patience.

**Turning point.** "I'll simply stop reading it."

**Consequence.** Pike's arc closes for Book One.

**Location.** The morning room.

**Characters.** Alexander, Pike.

**Weather and light.** Grey frost.

**Mood.** Understated.

**Date and time of day.** Monday 3 January 1853, late morning.

**Time since previous scene.** About three days.

**Scene duration.** About twenty minutes.

**Carried forward.** 3.1.

**Setups and plants.** Pike's return in later books.

**Rules to honor.** The public account.

**Target length.** About 600 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 25.3**

**Continuity in.** About three days after 25.2. Monday 3 January 1853, late morning. A grey frost. Inspector Pike has called. Alexander receives him in the morning room.

**Continuity out.** Pike has chosen his own incuriosity. "I'll not close the file. I'll simply stop reading it." His arc closes for Book One.

**Pike (returning).**

* About forty, heavy-lidded, greying moustache, plain brown coat too warm for the room. Too unhurried for the occasion; carries a notebook he rarely consults because he remembers everything. Patient questions, exact repetition of contradictions, never raises his volume.  
* He was told in 3.1 that Catherine was taken, and he has written twice monthly for fourteen months and walked past the gate twice a month. He sets down a question the way a man sets down a stone he means to pick up again.  
* Now he is given the public account: a fever contracted abroad, in a country left unnamed. He listens. He looks at the young Duke, at the black crape, at the carefully tidied wall of a story. He decides he would rather not officially know where this leads.  
* Line (Supporting Bible and outline): "I'll not close the file. I'll simply stop reading it." Understated.

**Alexander (POV).** Wants Pike to let it go. Quiet baritone. Gives the account in a level voice; it is true in its shape (Queen: "near enough to the truth's shape", 26.1). Does not lie beyond the account. Hand steady.

**Location.** The morning room. Grey frost at the windows.

**Rules in play.**

* The public account: fever contracted abroad, country unnamed. No bodies: buried abroad (25.1).  
* Pike is never read in. He chooses incuriosity. Do not have him guess the hidden world.  
* Ivor Vane's firm goes on paying rates on the burned tract, the loose thread Pike chooses not to pull (Supporting Bible). Do not surface Vane here.

**Threads touched.** Pike's return in later books. Chapter 3, scene 1 (his open file). The public account. The lie under a household.

**Flags.**

* **GAP:** the outline does not say what Pike is told about Catherine, who was missing fourteen months and whose file he holds. **DECIDED (default):** Pike does not raise her and Alexander does not volunteer. Pike's "simply stop reading it" covers the whole file, Catherine's included. Ask Fox whether the public account should say she has been found (and how).  
* The date: 3 January is a Monday; the outline says "late morning." Treat as fixed.  
* Pike's "brown coat too warm for the room" (3.1) should recur.

#### **Scene 4\. The siege at home**

**Scene summary.** Thomas reports what the boggart child told them: someone tested the thinning ward on the fen-side wall while the family was away. The prints were a gentleman's boots, not a poacher's, and they had walked the wall's whole length as if measuring it. It was an Institute watcher. Alexander understands that Silas's long siege has already come home. He asks Thomas to be steward in fact as well as in title, with O'Brien's blessing. The scene brings the Institute's pressure into the Sterlings' own house for the books to come, and carries Thomas's arc to its Book One close.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants to know about the wall.

**Obstacle.** None.

**Turning point.** The Institute is at the walls.

**Consequence.** Thomas becomes steward.

**Location.** The fen-side wall.

**Characters.** Alexander, Thomas, O'Brien.

**Weather and light.** A thaw, with mud.

**Mood.** Grim.

**Date and time of day.** Tuesday 4 January 1853, afternoon.

**Time since previous scene.** About a day.

**Scene duration.** About thirty minutes.

**Carried forward.** 12.5.

**Setups and plants.** Silas's siege (Book Two).

**Target length.** About 700 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 25.4**

**Continuity in.** About a day after 25.3. Tuesday 4 January, afternoon, a thaw. Thomas has asked Alexander to see something. The boggart child has been translated by Mrs. Ashworth (12.5).

**Continuity out.** The Institute is at the walls. Thomas is steward in fact, with O'Brien's blessing.

**Thomas (returning).**

* Forties, big-handed, fair and freckled, coat too formal for the work he does in it. Local Norfolk voice, growing more direct with responsibility. "Most estates, a man goes lame, he goes without wage the same week." He may say "Easy now."  
* Reports: someone tested the thinning ward on the fen-side wall while the family was away. The prints were a gentleman's boots, not a poacher's, and walked the wall's whole length as if measuring it. The boggart child did not make them and will not go near that wall (12.5).  
* Wants to be steward in truth. Alexander asks him.

**Mr. O'Brien.** Sixty, spare, silver-haired, precise and formal, hard to surprise. Gives his blessing in a sentence or a nod. No independent arc.

**Alexander (POV).** Wants to know about the wall. Reads the mud like a document. Understands Silas's long siege has already come home. Hand steady. He asks; he does not command.

**Location.** The fen-side wall. A thaw, mud. Harrow's Fen beyond (eleven tenant families, Edward would not sell).

**Rules in play.**

* The thinning ward (12.5) is real and has held only because of the Sterling wards and the boggarts' consent to protection (Supporting Bible).  
* The intruder was an Institute watcher. Alexander infers it; nobody has proof. The Institute's siege: observation, cultivation, questions rather than accusations (Location Bible).  
* Silas's strategy is Book Two. Do not have anyone name Silas.

**Threads touched.** Silas's siege (Book Two). Thomas's arc, acting to full steward. The boggart family and the Fen. The wards at home.

**Flags.**

* Whether O'Brien is present is not in the outline's action but is in its character list. **DECIDED:** he arrives at the end and gives his blessing in one line.  
* The "gentleman's boots": **DECIDED:** no name, no maker, only the length and the stride.

* Quick, forgettable, ink ground into his right hand, dry humor sparingly. Spins a stamped metal token on the table while he thinks (Supporting Bible, 6.4). Runs land routes: cattle van, drovers' road, smuggler's path.

He has a crate-marking boy at a goods siding in the Fens to see about: Davey Coop, twelve, chalk-fingered, father dead, mother takes in washing (7.1). He has paid for Davey all winter; Alexander saw it at 7.1.

#### **Scene 6\. The binder's hands**

**Scene summary.** In the sanctuary beneath the castle, Calum sits among the Sterling library's damaged books with a new silver scar over his heart where the Mark was. He is fed by cut and cup, and is gentler with the books than with himself. The Mark's hold is broken. It cannot be restored; the scar is all that remains of it. Niall sits with him and they talk in Old Town Scots, and Niall laughs, a sound Alexander has heard perhaps a dozen times in seven years. The scene places Calum after Book One and keeps his werewolf nature and his family in reserve for later books.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants Calum settled.

**Obstacle.** Nothing but the open future.

**Turning point.** Niall laughs.

**Consequence.** Calum's place is fixed.

**Characters.** Alexander, Niall, Calum.

**Weather and light.** Underground.

**Mood.** Gentle.

**Date and time of day.** Thursday 6 January 1853 (Twelfth Night), evening.

**Time since previous scene.** About a day.

**Scene duration.** About twenty minutes.

**Carried forward.** 23.3.

**Setups and plants.** The werewolf secret, and the Mark's future.

**Target length.** About 600 words.

**Continuity out.** Calum's place is fixed. Niall laughs. The werewolf secret and the Mark's future are kept in reserve.

**Calum (returning).**

* Slight, wiry, dark brown curls, freckles ghostly under the pallor, grey eyes that will not meet anyone's. Old Town Scots, hoarse. His hands close and open (careful, a binder's hands). He sits among the damaged books of the Sterling library with a silver scar over his heart where the Mark was. He is fed by cut and cup. He is gentler with the books than with himself.

**Alexander (POV).** Wants Calum settled. Watches from the edge. Says little. The half-smile may return. He does not intrude.

* The outline does not say who feeds him. **DECIDED:** Seraphina or Mrs. Ashworth, offstage.

#### **Scene 7\. The jar**

**Scene summary.** Catherine's room. The pin-money jar still sits in the false-bottomed box, and the atlas, salt-stained, is back on the table where she can reach it. She opens it to the Danube and begins, out loud, to plan a route she means to take for herself. She will have to travel by night and closed carriage, and she laughs at that before the joke is finished. The scene closes Catherine's Book One arc: she keeps her lightness, and trades escape for adventure she chooses.

**Perspective and goal.** Catherine. She wants a life of her own choosing.

**Scene duration.** About twenty minutes.

**Rules to honor.** Her lightness, kept as a choice.

**Target length.** About 700 words.

### **Chapter 26: The Hidden Crown**

*Windsor Castle and Sterling Castle, January 1853*

**CHAPTER OVERVIEW**

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants to take up his place on terms he can live with.

**Obstacle.** The Crown's expectations, Sebastian, and his unearned standing.

**Turning point.** "For Crown and Kin" spoken between equals.

**Consequence.** He ends the novel with the watch wound, the ball still a mystery, and Niall beside him. Something waits in the north.

**Span and length.** Tuesday 11 January to Thursday 13 January 1853\. Target about 4,400 words.

#### **Scene 1\. The Queen**

**Scene summary.** At Windsor, in a private room, Queen Victoria receives Alexander in plain dark silk, still in the court's mourning for Wellington. She gives him the public account of his parents' deaths herself, a fever contracted abroad, and calls it near enough to the truth's shape. She confirms Lady Beatrice Vale as Acting Commander until he has learned and earned command, and the title is never regent, because he is not a minor. His formal confirmation before the Crown and council will follow. At the end, lightly, she remarks that a young duke will be expected to marry and produce an heir, and Alexander bows and says nothing. The scene is Crown recognition of the succession, the institutional gap between title and standing, and the first planting of the marriage pressure.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants recognition.

**Obstacle.** Royal protocol.

**Turning point.** The mention of an heir.

**Consequence.** The marriage pressure is planted.

**Location.** A private room at Windsor Castle.

**Characters.** Alexander, Queen Victoria, Beatrice.

**Weather and light.** A clear, freezing morning.

**Mood.** Formal.

**Date and time of day.** Tuesday 11 January 1853, late morning.

**Time since previous scene.** About four days.

**Scene duration.** About thirty minutes.

**Carried forward.** The public account.

**Setups and plants.** Marriage pressure across the series.

**Rules to honor.** The court keeps the winter at Windsor Castle.

**Target length.** About 900 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 26.1**

**Continuity in.** About four days after 25.7. Tuesday 11 January 1853, late morning, a clear freezing morning. Alexander has come to Windsor by rail with Beatrice, in mourning black, wearing his father's coat over it or beneath a court coat (**DECIDED:** the coat, under a plain black overcoat, so the signet in the breast pocket (20.5) is on him). The public account (25.3) stands.

**Continuity out.** The Crown has recognized the succession. Beatrice is confirmed Acting Commander, never regent. The gap between title and standing is now institutional. Marriage pressure is planted and unanswered.

**First appearance: Queen Victoria.**

* Born 1819, 33 in 1852\. A little under five feet, made larger by the economy of her presence. Plain dark silk with a single mourning brooch at her throat: the court is in mourning for the Duke of Wellington, who died the previous September. Formal and exact when speaking as the Crown; otherwise dry and unhurried. She never needs to raise her voice.  
* She receives him in a private room. She gives the public account of his parents' deaths herself, "a fever contracted abroad," and calls it near enough to the truth's shape (Supporting Bible). She is the formal head of the Vanguard and knows everything.  
* She confirms Lady Beatrice Vale as Acting Commander "until he has learned and earned command." She does not use the word "regent"; he is not a minor. His formal confirmation before the Crown and council will follow.  
* At the end, lightly, she remarks that a young duke will be expected to marry and produce an heir. Alexander bows and says nothing. Do not have her press it.

**Beatrice.** Practical wool, iron-grey coil, the ward-worked brooch under her thumb. Stands to one side. Does not speak unless the Queen asks. Correct with the Crown, plain with Alexander. Still not softened.

**Alexander (POV).** Wants recognition and gets it, in a form he did not choose. Quiet baritone; fingertips still. Bows exactly. He does not thank the Queen for the account. His hand does not go to the pocket; the signet has been steady.

**Location.** A private room in the royal apartments at Windsor Castle. Clear freezing morning, winter light, a fire. The court keeps the winter at Windsor.

**Rules in play.**

* The reigning monarch is the Vanguard's formal head. The Duke of Norwich is hereditary operational second-in-command, answering only to the Crown (Series Bible).  
* Authority passes at Edward's death, but Alexander's standing lags: Beatrice is Acting Commander "never Regent."  
* Prince Albert is not to be revealed as a Vanguard patron until Book Four. Do not mention him beyond a passing consort, if at all.  
* The Queen's Vanguard authority is not to be explained to a reader who has not been told. Let her speak of "the Office" or "the service" as the scene needs.

**Threads touched.** The public account. Marriage pressure across the series. Title vs. standing (19.2, 20.4). Beatrice's price (20.4, 21.4). Sebastian (26.2).

**Flags.**

* The outline gives the Queen no name for the Vanguard's public face. **DECIDED:** the Office of Unusual Affairs (Series Bible) is used aloud only if needed.  
* Niall is not listed and not present. **DECIDED:** he stayed at Sterling Castle (with Catherine and Calum) because the audience is for the Duke. Not explained.  
* Whether the audience is one-on-one: the outline lists Beatrice. **DECIDED:** Beatrice is present in the room.

#### **Scene 2\. Sebastian**

**Scene summary.** In the corridor afterward, Sebastian congratulates him with perfect courtesy and a phrase about how much the council looks forward to guiding him. Alexander hears the threat in it. The scene marks Sebastian's turn in Book One: he now sees Alexander as a political threat.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants to leave.

**Obstacle.** Sebastian.

**Turning point.** He hears the threat.

**Consequence.** Sebastian is set as a threat.

**Location.** A corridor at Windsor.

**Characters.** Alexander, Sebastian.

**Weather and light.** Indoors.

**Mood.** Veiled menace.

**Date and time of day.** Tuesday 11 January 1853, about noon.

**Time since previous scene.** About ten minutes.

**Scene duration.** About five minutes.

**Carried forward.** 21.5.

**Setups and plants.** Book Two.

**Target length.** About 500 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 26.2**

**Continuity in.** About ten minutes after 26.1. Tuesday 11 January, about noon. Alexander has left the private room. Beatrice has gone ahead to speak with an attendant. Sebastian waits in the corridor.

**Continuity out.** Sebastian is set as a political threat. Alexander has heard it and says nothing.

**Sebastian (returning).**

* Fair-skinned, prematurely grey, groomed, still. Courtesy with something harder beneath. A ledger or a folio in one hand (**DECIDED:** ledger, back in his hand; it went missing at 18.1 and was never explained).  
* Congratulates him with perfect courtesy. Says how much the council looks forward to "guiding" him. Alexander hears the threat: the political antagonist's opening move. He may use his phrase "Until it doesn't" once, or not at all (he has used it under pressure at 19.2, 21.2).  
* He does not mention the letter (21.5), the ledger's absence, or the croft.

**Alexander (POV).** Wants to leave. Hears the threat. Does not answer it. Files it as he files a feather. Quiet baritone if he speaks: three or four words at most. He does not accuse.

**Location.** A corridor at Windsor. Indoors: stone, gaslight or lamps, a window with frost.

**Rules in play.**

* Sebastian frames ambition as concern. He is the political antagonist and must sound reasonable.  
* His Institute tie is not revealed. He keeps Ashcroft survey material openly under the Collectors-faction exchange agreement (Main Bible). Do not hint further.  
* Nobody accuses anyone. The scene ends on Alexander's understanding, not action.

**Threads touched.** Sebastian's arc (Books Two to Four). The council fracture. The letter (21.5). Book Two.

**Flags.**

* The ledger: **DECIDED:** he has it again. Nobody remarks on it.  
* Whether Sebastian attended the audience: not listed. **DECIDED:** no; he came to Windsor separately for the council.

#### **Scene 3\. Beatrice**

**Scene summary.** On the stair, Beatrice tells him what she needs from him in the months to come: training, field record, patience with procedure he will hate, and the discipline of waiting when waiting is right and refusing to wait when it is not. It is not warmth. It is an offer of terms, and he accepts them. The scene gives the political thread the shape it needs for Book Two.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants the terms.

**Obstacle.** None.

**Turning point.** He accepts them.

**Consequence.** The political shape for Book Two.

**Location.** A stair at Windsor.

**Characters.** Alexander, Beatrice.

**Weather and light.** Indoors.

**Mood.** Businesslike.

**Date and time of day.** Tuesday 11 January 1853, about half past twelve.

**Time since previous scene.** About twenty minutes.

**Scene duration.** About ten minutes.

**Carried forward.** 24.5.

**Setups and plants.** His training.

**Target length.** About 500 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 26.3**

**Continuity in.** About twenty minutes after 26.2. Tuesday 11 January, about half past twelve. Alexander has been walked toward a stair. Beatrice is waiting on it.

**Continuity out.** Beatrice's terms are stated and accepted. The political shape of Book Two is set.

**Beatrice.**

* Practical wool, iron-grey coil, brooch under her thumb. Not warm. Businesslike, plain. She says what she needs from him in the months to come: training, a field record, patience with procedure he will hate, and the discipline of waiting when waiting is right and refusing to wait when it is not.  
* This is an offer of terms. It is not softening. "You'll do" (24.5) is behind them; this is what "do" costs.  
* She does not touch the brooch here, or once.

**Alexander (POV).** Wants the terms. Accepts them. He does not argue, and does not thank her. Quiet baritone. Notes the last term (waiting when right, refusing when not) as the flaw he has just corrected at 22.6, and says nothing about it.

**Location.** A stair at Windsor. Indoors: stone, lamplight, the hush of a palace.

**Rules in play.**

* Beatrice is Acting Commander until the Crown says otherwise (19.2, 26.1).  
* Her price at 20.4 was "a plan I can defend to the Crown"; her terms here are the next price.  
* The Vanguard's discipline: field record earns command, not ceremony (Series Bible).

**Threads touched.** His training (Book Two). The title-standing wound (19.2). Beatrice's conditional trust. The council.

**Flags.**

* The Location Bible says a stair scene at Windsor is with Seraphina. The outline says Beatrice. **DECIDED:** Beatrice, per the outline. Seraphina appears at 26.4.  
* The terms are listed in the outline as four items. **DECIDED:** state them as a single speech at most; do not list them.

#### **Scene 4\. For Crown and Kin**

**Scene summary.** Home. In Edward's study, "For Crown and Kin" is carved into the stone above the hearth. In the council chamber below, two chairs stand empty. Beatrice, Seraphina, and Harcourt rise one by one. Alexander speaks the words, and Niall answers them, the exchange he once shared with Edward, now between the two of them. The scene is the Hook's secrecy answered by an open oath, and gives the book's title-phrase its closing ring.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants to speak the oath.

**Obstacle.** The two empty chairs.

**Turning point.** Niall answers.

**Consequence.** The Hook's secrecy is answered with an open oath.

**Location.** The council chamber beneath Sterling Castle.

**Characters.** Alexander, Niall, Beatrice, Seraphina, Harcourt.

**Weather and light.** Underground.

**Mood.** Solemn.

**Date and time of day.** Thursday 13 January 1853, evening.

**Time since previous scene.** About two days.

**Scene duration.** About twenty minutes.

**Carried forward.** The carving (2.1).

**Setups and plants.** Book Two.

**Rules to honor.** The exchange from the prequel.

**Target length.** About 700 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 26.4**

**Continuity in.** About two days after 26.3. Thursday 13 January, evening, about half past six. Alexander is at Sterling Castle. He comes down from Edward's study, where the carving "For Crown and Kin" stands over the hearth (2.1), to the council chamber beneath the keep. Sebastian is not present.

**Alexander (POV).**

* Wants to speak the oath. Obstacle: the two empty chairs (Edward's and Amelia's). He walks in his father's coat, signet in the pocket.

**Niall.** Grey, ribs still bound. Stands at Alexander's side, not behind. His answer is quiet, plain, and complete. The burr thickens. He does not look away.

**Beatrice, Seraphina, Harcourt.** Rise in that order. Beatrice with her brooch, Seraphina aged and upright with the amulet, dead eye toward the empty chairs, Harcourt with her spectacles and her notebook shut. Each says nothing or one short line. **DECIDED:** none says the oath aloud. They stand.

**Location.** The council chamber beneath Sterling Castle. A long table scarred by a century of tea rings and ink. Two chairs empty. Underground, lamplight.

**Rules in play.**

* The study vs. the chamber: the outline mentions both. **DECIDED:** the scene opens with Alexander's glance at the study carving on the way down, one line, then the chamber.

#### **Scene 5\. Equals**

**Scene summary.** Afterward, in the lower training yard where it began, Alexander asks Niall to stand beside him in the work: not behind him as his father's operative, not ahead of him as his teacher, but beside him. Niall says yes. As he puts his notebook away a folded handkerchief slips from between its pages, embroidered with a swallow. It is Catherine's gift from the festival, kept there since Chapter 1\. Niall picks it up and puts it back without a word, and Alexander sees it and says nothing. "Tomorrow, then," one of them says. The scene gives the pair their ending: chosen equals, no confession, and one more thing each chooses not to say.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants Niall beside him.

**Obstacle.** Everything unsaid.

**Turning point.** The handkerchief falls.

**Consequence.** Chosen equals, with one more thing unsaid.

**Location.** The lower training yard.

**Characters.** Alexander, Niall.

**Weather and light.** Frost and a thin moon.

**Mood.** Tender.

**Date and time of day.** Thursday 13 January 1853, about eight at night.

**Time since previous scene.** About ninety minutes.

**Scene duration.** About twenty minutes.

**Carried forward.** 1.2.

**Setups and plants.** The romance arc across the series.

**Rules to honor.** No confession.

**Target length.** About 700 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 26.5**

**Continuity in.** About ninety minutes after 26.4. Thursday 13 January, about eight at night. Alexander and Niall walk down to the lower training yard, where the lessons began. Frost.

**Continuity out.** Chosen equals, no confession, one more thing each chooses not to say. "Tomorrow, then."

**Alexander (POV).**

* Wants Niall beside him. Asks him to stand beside him in the work: not behind him as his father's operative, not ahead of him as his teacher, but beside him. Quiet baritone; plain; no flourish.  
* Niall says yes.  
* He sees the handkerchief fall and says nothing. He does not pick it up.

**Niall.**

* As he puts away his notebook (Alasdair Fraser's field notebook, 13.6, 24.6) a folded handkerchief slips from between its pages: embroidered with a swallow. It is Catherine's festival gift (1.2), kept there since Chapter 1\. He picks it up and puts it back without a word. The burr thickens if he speaks.  
* "Tomorrow, then," one of them says. **DECIDED:** Niall says it, as in the prequel (Location Bible: the phrase originates in the lower yard), and Alexander answers with the same two words.

**Location.** The lower training yard: the butt of the old chestnut used as a target, a bench against the wall. Frost.

**Rules in play.**

* No confession (Series Bible, Main Bible).  
* Alexander's own swallow handkerchief was used to bind Niall's ribs at 18.2. This is Niall's own. Do not confuse them.  
* The handkerchief's meaning is not spelled out: it is Catherine's gift; that it is Niall's to keep is enough.  
* "Tomorrow, then" originates here in the prequel; it is the series' small ritual.

**Threads touched.** The romance's arc across the series. The notebook (Book Two cipher). The handkerchiefs (1.2, 18.2). The lower yard (1.x, 6.x, prequel 7). "Tomorrow, then."

**Flags.**

* The outline's weather says "a thin moon." A waxing crescent about two to three days old (new moon about 10 to 11 January, calculated) sets about two to three hours after sunset, near 6:30 pm, so at eight it has set. **DECIDED:** keep frost and the lantern; drop the moon or say only that the sky is clear and moonless. Confirm with Fox.  
* Whose line is "Tomorrow, then": the outline says "one of them." **DECIDED:** Niall speaks it, Alexander returns it.  
* The yard at night: **DECIDED:** a lantern hung on the wall bracket, lit by Niall.

#### **Scene 6\. The watch, and the cairn**

**Scene summary.** That evening, at the hour his father always did, Alexander winds Edward's watch for the first time. It has been weeks. In the other pocket of the coat, the flattened rifle ball sits where he left it, and he still does not know what it is. The last image is far to the north. Snow lies on an ancient cairn in the heather, and in the dark inside it, something tends a husk. Beneath Catherine's collarbone, five hundred miles to the south, the bite is cold and quiet. For now. The scene gives Alexander his grounding and plants the series' long return of Hessian, measured in Catherine's body.

**Perspective and goal.** Alexander. He wants to go on.

**Obstacle.** None.

**Turning point.** He winds the watch.

**Consequence.** The novel ends.

**Location.** Edward's study, then the northern cairn.

**Characters.** Alexander, Josef (unseen), Catherine (referenced).

**Weather and light.** A still night.

**Mood.** Quiet, with a threat under it.

**Date and time of day.** Thursday 13 January 1853, the hour Edward always wound it.

**Time since previous scene.** About an hour.

**Scene duration.** About ten minutes.

**Carried forward.** 18.4.

**Setups and plants.** Hessian's return in the series.

**Rules to honor.** The watch and the ball. The pull quiet for now.

**Target length.** About 500 words.

##### **SCENE PACKET 26.6**

**Continuity in.** About an hour after 26.5. Thursday 13 January, about nine at night, the hour Edward always wound his watch (14.6, decided as nine). A still night. Alexander sits alone in his father's study.

**Continuity out.** The novel ends. The watch is wound, the ball unresolved, Niall beside him, something waiting in the north.

**Alexander (POV).**

* Wants to go on. In Edward's study, the carving above the hearth. He takes the watch from the coat (stopped since the flood, 17.4, 18.4) and winds it for the first time. It has been weeks. He does it at the hour his father always did. The gesture is small, exact and private. **DECIDED:** he sets the hands by the study's own clock or by the window's light; do not invent the clock's make.  
* In the other pocket the flattened rifle ball sits where he left it. He still does not know what it is. **DECIDED:** he does not take it out; he touches it through the cloth, no more.  
* The signet in the breast pocket: hand to it, steadying (24.6).  
* He does not weep. Quiet. Half-smile absent.

**The cairn (POV shift, seen not felt).**

* Far to the north: snow lies on an ancient cairn in the heather. In the dark inside it, something tends a husk (Josef, unseen and unnamed). Do not narrate Josef's face or Hessian's.

**Catherine (referenced).** Beneath her collarbone, five hundred miles to the south, the bite is cold and quiet. "For now." She is not on the page. The reader is told the pull is quiet, not gone.

**Location.** Edward's study: broad oak desk, tobacco and cold pipe ash, a fire, drawn curtains, "For Crown and Kin" carved above the hearth. Then the northern cairn: snow, heather, dark. A still night.

**Rules in play.**

* The watch: Edward wound it every evening at the same hour (Main Bible). Alexander winds it at nine (14.6 flag).  
* The ball: the flattened rifle ball of 1838 that killed Alasdair Fraser, cut from Edward's coat (Main Bible). Alexander does not know. Niall knows and has not said.  
* The pull is quiet but returning across the series: it stirs late in Book Three, strengthens in Book Four, returns in full in Book Five (Main Bible). Do not state any of it.  
* Josef is unseen. Hessian is not named in the cairn passage.

**Threads touched.** Hessian's return (Books Three to Five). The ball (Book Two). The watch (14.6, 18.4). The gesture. The whole novel's closing image.

**Flags.**

* The distance "five hundred miles": Loch Maree to Norfolk. **DECIDED:** keep the figure as the outline gives it.  
* Timing: sunrise-to-sunset does not matter here. The hour is nine at night, as in the 14.6 flag.  
* The last line's "For now" is the outline's. **DECIDED:** keep it as the closing note; do not add a further hook.
